12 Failure Recovery

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can an agent that records every step it takes still leave the world half changed?

A durable record tells a restarted agent what it intended and what it observed, but the record does not change the world. A provisioning request whose reply was lost may have created a billed environment or nothing at all, and no amount of replay settles which. Restoring a workspace snapshot resets local files, while the remote environment the agent created keeps running and keeps charging. Recovery therefore begins where recording ends, with decisions made on incomplete evidence. A runtime that reads a timeout as failure sends a second request and can leak a second resource, and one that reads it as success builds on an effect that may never have happened. Some effects can be undone only by a new operation that can itself fail, and some cannot be undone at all, so the cheapest place to stop a bad change is before the step that commits it. Other failures raise no error. A trajectory can alternate between two edits for dozens of turns, every call returning success and every health check green, while each turn resends a longer context and the bill grows faster than the turn count. Retries that every layer grants on its own allowance multiply against a dependency that is already failing, so one slow service can stall the shared worker pool. A warning in the prompt does not contain any of these hazards, because the model may ignore advice that a runtime bound would enforce. The runtime must record the cleanup it owes before it acts and keep unknown outcomes owned rather than guessed. It must weigh another attempt against unwinding by the cost still ahead rather than the cost already spent, and halt work that makes no progress at the gate where new actions are admitted. Over a long horizon, the authority an agent has already exercised becomes a set of obligations the runtime must settle.

Learning Objectives
  • Select among retry, repair, compensation, and escalation by an effect’s evidence state first, then admission, then expected cost
  • Register compensations before forward dispatch and order their execution with a dependency-based cleanup graph
  • Compare one bounded forward repair against compensation and rebuilding, in time and dollars, at the same endpoint
  • Analyze how checks placed before an irreversible commitment reduce expected loss while cleanup and residual duties remain
  • Construct progress detectors and price detection latency against a cumulative budget ceiling
  • Design dependency containment with a single retry owner, scoped circuit breakers, and a capped worker share
  • Design an incident containment order that limits blast radius while preserving evidence and cleanup
  • Evaluate recovery policies by accepted tasks, residual obligations, and unresolved outcomes rather than local status
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

12.1 Recovering After Partial Effects

Over a long horizon, authority becomes a recovery problem for the runtime.

When a replacement worker takes over the repair agent’s failed connection-pool repair (Four lighthouse workloads), it reconstructs the execution log and restores the candidate workspace snapshot ws_r18. The surviving history records an intent to run test_r18, but contains no evidence confirming whether the test passed, failed, or ever ran. As Resuming a Trajectory demonstrated, the worker cannot treat missing evidence as a clean slate; it must retain that uncertainty alongside the unresolved liability. State reconstruction recovers the factual foundation of the execution, but facts alone do not dictate the next step. The runtime’s recovery policy must decide whether to wait for delayed evidence, attempt a targeted repair of the candidate snapshot, or abort the task entirely.

Now expand the scope of the repair to include an external side effect. Besides the unresolved test_r18, which remains a separate operation whose outcome is still owed, the repair provisions an isolated, temporary integration-test environment and runs a new integration suite against ws_r18 inside it. The agent’s authorization grant covers this ephemeral environment and nothing else. Production deployments, public network routing, payment processing, and the deletion of unrelated cloud resources remain strictly beyond its authority.

If a network timeout or process crash drops the provisioning reply, the worker faces an immediate impasse: it cannot know whether the remote environment was successfully allocated or never created.1 Suppose instead that telemetry confirms creation, but the integration suite subsequently crashes. Rolling back the local workspace to snapshot ws_r18 resets internal state, yet the external environment remains active and billing charges continue to accumulate. Local rollback does not undo distributed side effects. Reclaiming those external resources requires an explicit compensating cleanup operation, followed by concrete evidence that the operation satisfied its declared postconditions.

1 Lost acknowledgment: Gray named this the generals paradox in his 1978 notes on database operating systems: over a channel that can lose messages, no finite exchange lets both parties know that the last message arrived (Gray 1978). Fischer, Lynch, and Paterson later proved that in a fully asynchronous system no deterministic consensus protocol can guarantee termination while preserving agreement and validity if even one process may crash, since a crashed process cannot be told apart from a slow one (Fischer et al. 1985). A tool-call timeout inherits the same ambiguity, which is why the settlement rule keeps the outcome unknown rather than guessing.

Gray, Jim. 1978. “Notes on Data Base Operating Systems.” In Operating Systems: An Advanced Course. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-08755-9_9.
Fischer, Michael J., Nancy A. Lynch, and Michael S. Paterson. 1985. “Impossibility of Distributed Consensus with One Faulty Process.” Journal of the ACM 32 (2): 374–82.

Durable logs, checkpoints, and deterministic replay reconstruct what the runtime intended and what it observed. That historical reconstruction provides the evidence for recovery, but passive logs cannot alter the outside world. An execution log does not tear down an orphaned test cluster, resolve a lost network acknowledgment, or evaluate whether a subsequent attempt justifies its operational cost.

↩︎ Durable logs, checkpoints, and deterministic replay are established in Durable Execution.

Complicating matters further, agent failures frequently manifest without throwing runtime exceptions. An agent trajectory can issue structurally valid tool calls while drifting into destructive patterns: alternating indefinitely between two conflicting edits, bombarding a degraded testing service with aggressive retries, or attempting to transmit private credentials after parsing an untrusted document. Every network call returns a successful status, every worker health check reports green, yet the agent either makes zero forward progress or actively threatens system integrity.

The recovery architecture records every compensating cleanup obligation before dispatching the operation that produces an external side effect. When an operation is interrupted, the runtime evaluates the surviving evidence alongside projected forward costs and ignores sunk expenses. It then chooses whether to retry the operation, repair the proposal within a bound, run compensating actions that unwind external side effects, or hand control to an authorized human operator. Furthermore, the architecture bounds ongoing executions even when no component raises an explicit error. By intercepting actions at the admission gate where new tool calls are scheduled, the runtime halts unproductive loops, throttles retries against saturated dependencies, and quarantines suspicious behaviors. It does not rely on prompt warnings that an agent model might overlook or ignore.

Section 12.2 demonstrates why the environment service and runtime log in this workflow share no atomic commit boundary, carrying forward from the harness the two rules that govern every subsequent recovery decision. Section 12.3 translates these invariants into an executable saga pattern, tracking compensation obligations as explicit, dependency-ordered records. Where an execution encounters partial failure, section 12.4 formalizes how to evaluate the marginal cost of forward repair against backward unwinding, while section 12.5 governs the critical transition across irreversible commitment points.

To address failures that emerge without explicit error signals, the runtime introduces three containment boundaries. Progress watchdogs identify trajectory stagnation and oscillatory loops (section 12.6). Dependency controls insulate shared backend services from retry cascades (section 12.7). Automated quarantine policies isolate suspected security incidents (section 12.8). Finally, section 12.9 unites these control loops in a recovery worksheet for a degraded connection-pool repair, detailing the fault-injection test harness required to verify recovery correctness under real-world failures.

12.2 Effects Outside a Shared Transaction

When the integration suite crashes inside the temporary test environment, rolling back the agent runtime’s local database cannot reach across the network to deallocate the remote virtual machine. Even if the cloud provider’s environment service executes internal database transactions with strict ACID guarantees, its database and the runtime’s execution log commit in separate administrative domains with no shared atomic boundary. Inside a single database or a tightly coupled cluster, a locking transaction holds exclusive locks until commit or abort, and two-phase commit coordinates an atomic decision across cooperating resource managers (Gray 1981). In two-phase commit, each participant first prepares by writing the recovery state needed to either commit or abort on command, and then holds its locked resources in that prepared state until the coordinator announces the global decision. Agent tool workflows break both requirements of that protocol. First, the external endpoints an agent invokes commit each request on receipt and rarely expose a prepare phase or accept the agent runtime as their transaction coordinator. Second, even where internal services do cooperate, a prepared participant must keep its resources locked while waiting for the coordinator, and a coordinator crash while participants are prepared prolongs that wait until recovery completes. Holding database locks or reserved capacity across model generation, long integration tests, or asynchronous human review would stall competing work for unacceptable periods. Across the independently administered services in this workflow, recovery therefore relies on tentative operations, explicit cancellations, and idempotent exchanges rather than assuming a shared transaction (Helland 2007).

2 Compensating transaction: Gray’s 1981 travel-agent example had this exact structure (Gray 1981). Because each flight or hotel reservation commits at an independent organization, canceling the itinerary requires executing a compensating transaction. Gray proposed that each nested step return the name and parameters of its compensator for the parent coordinator to log, falling back to human intervention as a last resort. However, Gray’s coordinator logs the compensator only after the forward step successfully returns. A trajectory saga must instead record the recovery obligation before dispatching the request over the network, because a dropped connection or lost acknowledgment would otherwise leave unmanaged side effects with no corresponding recovery record.

Gray, Jim. 1981. “The Transaction Concept: Virtues and Limitations.” Proceedings of the Seventh International Conference on Very Large Data Bases (VLDB) 7: 144–54.

When atomic rollback across independent boundaries is impossible, systems must rely on compensation:2 a forward-executing operation that establishes acceptable postconditions after a prior effect has already committed. Compensation does not erase history or restore the universe to its pristine pre-transaction state. For example, releasing a cloud-hosted development environment deallocates its virtual compute instances and stops active billing meters. The provisioning event, network ingress logs, and financial charges already accrued remain permanent, and the teardown request itself may fail on an unreachable network. To structure multi-step workflows without global isolation, Garcia-Molina and Salem introduced the saga pattern for long-lived transactions (Garcia-Molina and Salem 1987). Rather than treating the entire trajectory as a single atomic unit, a saga partitions execution into a sequence of smaller, independently committed steps. It pairs each forward step with a compensating action designed to mitigate its side effects if a later step fails.

↪ Two-phase commit’s blocking behavior and the original saga abstraction are developed in Classical Transaction Background.

Compensation is not the only recovery strategy. Apart from a plain retry of an operation whose repeat contract permits one, an agent runtime chooses among three families of response:

  1. Compensation: execute explicit counteracting operations to neutralize partial side effects.
  2. Forward repair: preserve valid intermediate artifacts, such as a successfully compiled binary or a provisioned database schema, and adjust subsequent steps rather than discarding completed progress.
  3. Escalated handoff: halt autonomous dispatch and transfer unresolved obligations, active resource handles, and diagnostic logs to an authorized human operator.

The runtime balances available evidence against projected forward costs to choose among these alternatives; capital and compute time already expended are sunk costs that must not distort future recovery decisions. Whichever path it selects, two rules that the harness already enforces govern the choice, and recovery is where both are tested hardest.

The settlement rule governs what the runtime may believe about an external effect. The harness stated it as a rule about the operation table: withdrawing an operation’s observation eligibility never settles the operation, and only authenticated, correlated evidence does. Recovery needs the rule in a sharper form, as three mutually exclusive evidence states for every dispatched operation: established, established absent, or unknown. An outcome begins as unknown, and only evidence correlated with that specific operation, interpreted through the target endpoint’s contract, can move it to established or established absent. A dropped TCP acknowledgment, an HTTP gateway timeout, an asynchronous accepted-but-pending status, or a status query that returns nothing while the original request may still be in transit all leave the outcome unknown. An unknown outcome keeps its liability reservation against the task budget, keeps a durable record with a named owner, and blocks any forward action that presupposes either success or failure. The runtime may repeat the dispatch only when the endpoint’s verified repeat contract makes re-execution safe, which is settlement before retry (principle 8).

↩︎ The settlement rule, which separates observation eligibility from settling the operation, is established in The Trajectory Record.

The admission check governs what the runtime may do next. Every candidate operation passes the same predicates the harness applies to ordinary work, whether it is forward progress, a retry, an environment repair, a status probe, or a cleanup action. The predicates are valid authority and approval, satisfied dependencies, available capacity, and enough time before the deadline. They also require a reservation whose bounded liability fits beside actual consumption, outstanding liability from unsettled operations, and the protected finalization reserve. The check is re-evaluated at the dispatch boundary immediately before transmission, so a plan-time approval or an earlier durable intent cannot override a later administrative hold, a revoked token, or a depleted budget. Recovery operations receive no exemption. Organizational liability for remote infrastructure does, however, outlive the agent’s reasoning loop, so administrative cleanup backed by independent operational credentials can still pass admission and decommission orphaned resources long after model-directed continuation has ended.

↩︎ The admission check and the finalization reserve it protects are established in Transition guards and the admission check.

A naive undo stack, such as a reversed list of tool invocations that maps server creation to server deletion, is too brittle to satisfy either the settlement rule or the admission check. In-flight operations fail asynchronously, remote endpoints drop responses, and partial writes leave external resources in states that cannot be resolved by replaying inverse functions with original arguments. Instead, the runtime must persist a structured recovery obligation before allowing any request to cross the network boundary. This obligation captures correlation identifiers, parent-child causal links, parameter snapshots, timeout thresholds, and settlement state, providing the durable bookkeeping needed to handle partial failure without leaking resources. The trajectory saga pattern formalizes this durable record of obligations.

12.3 The Trajectory Saga Pattern

When the repair worker dispatches its request to provision the temporary test environment, the remote service may allocate the virtual machine and start billing even if a network partition drops the return acknowledgment before it reaches the runtime. That lost reply breaks the binary assumption of a classical database saga, which coordinates a long-lived transaction across independent services by assuming that each local step either commits cleanly or aborts cleanly and reports which one happened. In an agent trajectory, the runtime can be left with an active cleanup obligation for a remote resource whose endpoint-assigned identifier has not yet arrived. A saga for agent tool calls therefore cannot merely log completed steps after they return; it must durably register each recovery obligation before dispatch and explicitly track what the runtime does not yet know.

Definition 12.1: Trajectory saga

Trajectory saga is a recovery pattern that durably records constituent tool actions and coordinates compensating actions when established external effects must be remediated, while explicitly preserving unresolved outcomes and inter-action dependencies.

  1. Significance: Enables systematic recovery across independent external tools and services that share no common distributed transaction boundary.
  2. Distinction: Compensation represents new forward execution that produces its own observable outcome, rather than an atomic rollback or physical reversal of the environment.
  3. Common pitfall: Deferring cleanup registration until after receiving a successful forward response, creating a crash window where external resources leak without any durable record of their recovery obligation.

In its classical formulation, a saga consists of a sequence of separately committed forward steps \(\SagaForwardStep_1, \dots, \SagaForwardStep_n\), each paired with a compensating step \(\SagaCompensatingStep_i\) that the runtime executes if downstream failures require remediating earlier effects (Garcia-Molina and Salem 1987). A forward action that produces no external side effect requires no compensation. When every forward step succeeds, the saga completes execution as \(\SagaForwardStep_1, \dots, \SagaForwardStep_n\). If step \(\SagaForwardStep_k\) fails, a linear saga unwinds by executing compensating actions in reverse order:

\[\SagaForwardStep_1, \dots, \SagaForwardStep_{k-1}, \SagaFailedForwardStep, \SagaCompensatingStep_k, \SagaCompensatingStep_{k-1}, \dots, \SagaCompensatingStep_1, \tag{1}\]

where \(\SagaCompensatingStep_k\) runs only when evidence confirms that the failed step actually produced an external effect. If the outcome of \(\SagaForwardStep_k\) remains unknown, the runtime’s settlement rules keep the obligation for \(\SagaCompensatingStep_k\) open rather than guessing. While equation 1 models an idealized sequential workflow, non-linear trajectories require a generalized directed cleanup graph. Each compensating step \(\SagaCompensatingStep_i\) is itself an independent, separately committed operation with its own execution outcome; an unwind can stall partway through, leaving certain compensations settled while others remain pending or in flight.

12.3.1 Obligation registration

Registering a compensating step before dispatching the forward action creates an immediate addressing problem: when the worker sends a provisioning request, the cloud service has not yet created the environment or returned the handle needed to tear it down. Waiting until the reply arrives to log the cleanup would open a crash window in which a dropped connection orphans a live, billed environment with no local recovery record; the runtime instead durably registers a cleanup template before forward dispatch and binds the target identifier when evidence arrives. In our temporary test-infrastructure workflow, let provision_test_env_r18 be the stable logical operation that provisions an isolated test environment, and let release_test_env_r18 be its corresponding cleanup operation, a distinct logical identity that remains stable across retries and restarts. Neither identity should be confused with the endpoint-generated environment identifier. That system handle, denoted env_r18_01 in a successful provisioning run, becomes known to the runtime only after correlated response evidence settles the forward operation.

The pre-dispatch cleanup template records the exact forward operation and payload, authorized capability scope, execution preconditions, dependencies, and an explicit rule for binding the returned environment identifier once it arrives. Appended directly to the write-ahead log alongside the forward admission record, this registration shares the forward attempt’s storage acknowledgment and resource reservation, avoiding the overhead and complexity of an independent state machine. Registering the cleanup before the effect it cleans up applies intent before effect (principle 11) to the recovery obligation itself.

↩︎ The durable intent record written before dispatch is established in Durable Intent Before Dispatch.

The resulting template binds cleanup to the specific environment instantiated by this provisioning operation, rather than relying on transient metadata like display names. Durably registering this template establishes a binding commitment for future recovery rather than asserting that the resource already exists. Table 1 follows this distinction across each phase of the crash boundary.

When an acknowledgment is lost following dispatch, an idempotent result-recovery probe may retrieve the missing outcome. For a mutating tool without endpoint deduplication, this is the probe that the tool contract of equation 7.1 ships beside its actuate and compensate routines. The template registered here instantiates that contract’s compensate routine for one specific, bound resource. If probing is unsupported or inconclusive, settlement rules mandate that the operation remain open with an assigned recovery owner. A naive runtime might query the external service, observe that the environment is absent, and prematurely close the cleanup record on the assumption that creation failed. Doing so creates a race condition. If the original provisioning request was merely delayed in transit, the remote service may instantiate the environment seconds later, leaving an orphaned resource that accrues compute charges indefinitely without any controlling process. If the endpoint supports explicit request cancellation, the runtime can invoke that contract to terminate the pending creation, provided the endpoint guarantees that canceled requests will not take effect downstream.

↪ Result recovery, as distinct from repeated execution, is developed in Repetition and Result Recovery.

12.3.2 Semantic compensation

Once env_r18_01 is bound and a failed integration run triggers release_test_env_r18, the runtime must verify that teardown succeeded even though the external world cannot be rewound to its state before provisioning. Rather than checking for an impossible physical reversal, the runtime checks whether the compensating action has re-established a declared set of postconditions while leaving outside side effects to their own lifecycles.

Definition 12.2: Semantic compensation

Semantic compensation is an independent forward action designed to establish declared application postconditions following an earlier side effect, without claiming to erase execution history or restore every dimension of the external environment.

  1. Significance: Provides a verifiable, checkable objective for recovery when exact physical or historical reversal is impossible.
  2. Distinction: Releasing a temporary environment terminates its active allocation; it does not un-send network packets, erase system audit logs, or refund consumed compute charges.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating an endpoint’s asynchronous acceptance response as proof that declared postconditions have been achieved when only request receipt has occurred.

In our running example, the compensation contract stipulates that the task-owned environment must no longer be allocated and that no active integration test may still depend on it, requiring any diagnostic artifacts to be transferred to durable storage prior to deallocation. The contract also defines what evidence the endpoint must supply to demonstrate successful release. Because final billing charges often settle asynchronously hours after an environment is torn down, the runtime decouples the physical resource obligation from financial settlement.

Formally, let \(\PreForwardEnvironmentState\) represent the state of the environment prior to executing forward step \(\SagaForwardStep\), and let \(\CompensatedEnvironmentState\) denote the state after executing \(\SagaForwardStep\) followed by its compensating action \(\SagaCompensatingStep\). Because physical time cannot be reversed and side effects alter audit trails and financial balances, \(\CompensatedEnvironmentState\) and \(\PreForwardEnvironmentState\) inevitably differ. Compensation guarantees equivalence only with respect to an explicitly declared set of postcondition predicates \(\CompensationPostconditionSet\). The compensation succeeds if and only if both states agree on every predicate in \(\CompensationPostconditionSet\):

\[\CompensatedEnvironmentState \equiv_{\CompensationPostconditionSet} \PreForwardEnvironmentState \iff \forall \CompensationPostcondition \in \CompensationPostconditionSet:\ \CompensationPostcondition(\CompensatedEnvironmentState) = \CompensationPostcondition(\PreForwardEnvironmentState). \tag{2}\]

Here, predicates such as “the environment is allocated” and “an admitted test depends on it” belong to \(\CompensationPostconditionSet\), and both evaluate to false in \(\PreForwardEnvironmentState\) and \(\CompensatedEnvironmentState\). In contrast, billing records and audit histories lie outside \(\CompensationPostconditionSet\), which is why financial reconciliation requires an independent settlement lifecycle. A compensation contract claims to satisfy only the predicates explicitly declared in \(\CompensationPostconditionSet\); unmodeled side effects remain unaddressed.

Compensation inevitably operates on an environment that has moved on. External services or human operators may have observed the intermediate state, consumed compute seconds cannot be un-billed, and concurrent processes can alter cleanup preconditions. For instance, if a peer workflow has attached to the temporary environment for joint debugging, the originating agent cannot unilaterally destroy the resource simply because its own plan stalled. The runtime’s admission control boundary must inspect live ownership and active dependency graphs at the moment of compensation, rather than blindly following stale planning text.

Local workspace restoration plays a complementary but narrower role. A compatible checkpoint restores only the local workspace projection managed directly by the runtime. Checkpoints cannot reset remote cloud environments, running external daemons, dispatched batch jobs, or accrued service charges. Those external side effects demand semantic compensation. When recovery requires both local rollback and remote compensation, the runtime orchestrates both mechanisms, verifying the independent outcome of each path before declaring the system recovered.

↩︎ Checkpoint restoration, which reinstates the record and workspace without re-executing external actions, is established in Consistent recovery boundaries.

12.3.3 Cleanup failures

Because cleanup actions cross the same external tool boundaries as forward actions, they encounter the same failure modes: network drops, rate limits, and remote service outages. Consequently, every compensating operation must pass admission control, and its intent must be durably committed before dispatch. When retrying a failed cleanup call, the runtime assigns each transport attempt a unique attempt identifier, while release_test_env_r18 continues to designate the persistent logical operation. Retries adhere to the endpoint’s repeat contract. The stable logical key ensures that duplicate deletion requests execute safely without targeting a different resource if the external system reuses names or identifiers.

↩︎ Stable operation identities, with a fresh attempt identity for each retry under the endpoint’s repeat contract, are established in Operation identity and endpoint contracts.

The coordinator retires a compensation entry only upon receiving verifiable outcome evidence, rather than assuming success simply because a remote call returned without a transport error. If a release call times out, three states remain possible: the environment may be gone, it may still be undergoing deallocation, or it may remain allocated. The settlement rules keep all three possibilities open. Furthermore, cleanup actions consume quota and wall-clock execution budget; a timed-out call refunds nothing. If cleanup cannot progress further within its assigned budget, the obligation stays on record with its accumulated evidence and an assigned escalation owner. In this way, a failed agent trajectory can terminate safely without masking unresolved external leaks.

Preregistered cleanup templates execute under the supervision of the trusted runtime coordinator without invoking another model inference step. This separation enforces least-privilege control: even if the model’s forward execution authority is revoked following an anomaly or budget exhaustion, a narrowly scoped cleanup grant remains accessible to the coordinator to tear down provisioned resources. If remediation requires operations beyond this pre-authorized grant, the runtime does not guess; it halts and issues an explicitly owned handoff. Later authenticated evidence or manual operator actions can settle the obligation without restarting the obsolete agent trajectory.

12.3.4 The cleanup graph

Tearing down env_r18_01 the moment a failure is detected would sever an integration test that is still running inside it and destroy diagnostic logs before they are copied to durable storage, whereas deleting an unrelated local scratch directory need not wait for either step. A simple last-in, first-out (LIFO) unwind stack is adequate only when an agent executes a strictly linear sequence and every cleanup precondition follows that reverse order. When an agent invokes tools concurrently, triggers asynchronous background tasks, or branches across speculative plans, recovery requires tracking explicit causal dependencies in a directed cleanup graph \((\CleanupGraphVertexSet,\CleanupGraphEdgeSet)\), where each vertex represents an admitted cleanup operation and a directed edge

\[\SagaCompensatingStep_j \rightarrow \SagaCompensatingStep_k\]

signifies that compensating operation \(\SagaCompensatingStep_j\) must establish its declared settlement conditions before \(\SagaCompensatingStep_k\) is permitted to dispatch. Declared cleanup dependencies must form a directed acyclic graph (DAG); cyclical dependencies represent contradictory recovery requirements that demand explicit operator intervention rather than an arbitrary stack ordering.

In our temporary environment scenario, confirming that the test process has stopped (or canceling it and verifying termination) and preserving diagnostic test artifacts outside the sandbox are both predecessors of release_test_env_r18 in \((\CleanupGraphVertexSet,\CleanupGraphEdgeSet)\). Removing the unrelated local scratch directory forms an independent vertex with no edges to the environment release, provided no active test or pending evidence collection depends on it. The cleanup graph answers what must happen first, settlement rules determine what has been empirically verified, and admission control determines which operations may run next. The graph cannot be sequenced until provisioning evidence has bound its target, which is why figure 1 draws the release ordering beneath the registration step it depends on.

Structuring cleanup as a DAG isolates failures and enables concurrent unwinding. An unresolved or failed cleanup node blocks only its transitive dependents in \((\CleanupGraphVertexSet,\CleanupGraphEdgeSet)\); independent recovery subgraphs can proceed immediately once their predecessors settle, their preconditions hold, and they clear admission control. Executing independent cleanup actions in parallel reduces overall recovery latency.

The cleanup graph enforces ordering without erasing historical accountability. Settled nodes retain their verification evidence, while failed or ambiguous nodes remain bound to their assigned owners. While an authorized administrator may deliberately accept residual risk to unblock downstream execution, the system never silently records an unconfirmed release as clean.

12.3.5 Branch-scoped compensation

If an agent creates a cloud test sandbox during a common trajectory prefix before branching into three candidate test configurations, tearing down that sandbox when Candidate B fails would sabotage the active experiments in Candidates A and C. During tree search or multi-candidate exploration, branch ancestry tracks where model execution diverged, but it reveals nothing about which external resources each branch still claims.

The runtime therefore decouples resource ownership from logical branch history by tracking effects and cleanup obligations through reference-counted dependency graphs. When the runtime abandons a speculative branch, it reclaims only those resources owned exclusively by that branch, once its outstanding work has settled or stopped cleanly. Shared resources remain allocated until all surviving dependencies permit release. Furthermore, if an abandoned branch dispatched a provisioning request whose outcome remains unknown, that request stays recorded in the durable operation table; pruning a branch from the scheduler does not erase an unresolved external obligation.

Restoring a branch’s local workspace or context window does not rewind the external environment to the branch point, and evidence gathered from an abandoned candidate cannot be assumed valid for a sibling branch merely because they share an ancestor. During counterfactual execution, local workspaces can be rolled back to earlier checkpoints, but external networks, databases, and billing meters cannot. Enforcing this distinction ensures that speculative branch pruning never silently discards external recovery duties.

↩︎ Counterfactual branches, which replay a recorded prefix and then require fresh observations, are established in Counterfactual branches.

Checkpoint 12.1: Compensation obligations and dependency scope

Compensation is registered before dispatch and settled by evidence afterward. Each prompt places an obligation on one side of that boundary.

While semantic compensation systematically remediates partial effects, unwinding external work is not always the right response to a fault. When earlier steps have already built healthy, reusable state and a subsequent step fails before touching it, tearing those resources down wastes time and budget that a bounded in-place fix could preserve. Deciding whether to unwind or repair in place requires comparing the expected cost of forward correction against the expense of backward unwinding and rebuilding.

12.4 Forward Recovery

Take the branch in which provisioning evidence did arrive. The separately authorized operation provision_test_env_r18 has settled, binding the temporary integration-test environment env_r18_01. Before dispatching the integration test, the runtime validates the proposed test configuration and rejects it because a field requiring an integer contains a string. The candidate workspace remains ws_r18, and no integration test has started. Releasing the environment through release_test_env_r18 would discard expensive, fully functional preparation. Conversely, a bounded correction of the configuration can restore readiness without rebuilding the environment from scratch.

This situation differs from the unresolved test_r18 intent that the replacement worker recovered. When the runtime rejects a proposal before dispatch, it establishes definitive evidence that the executor never launched the action. A missing result under the settlement rule provides no such assurance; the operation might have succeeded, failed, or stalled midway through execution.

In dependable system design, forward recovery modifies the current state or repairs a proposed action to restore an admissible path toward completing the task. By contrast, backward recovery returns the system to an earlier saved state. The classical dependability taxonomy formalizes these strategies as rollforward and rollback, where rollback restores a previously captured checkpoint (Avizienis et al. 2004).3 Forward repair, the response family named earlier, is the agent runtime’s form of forward recovery, which keeps valid intermediate artifacts and corrects the proposal that failed. In an agentic system, restoring a workspace checkpoint performs backward recovery for the local filesystem and memory state that the checkpoint covers. External systems, however, rarely support true rollback, and compensation sits across the divide. In intent it is backward recovery, because it moves the environment back toward the pre-effect predicates of equation 2. In execution it is forward, a new operation with its own outcome, which is why the trajectory saga definition calls it new forward execution. It achieves semantic equivalence rather than physical restoration.

3 Recovery vocabulary: The same terminology carries conflicting meanings across the two architectural lineages this chapter draws on. Garcia-Molina and Salem defined running compensations as backward recovery for sagas, reserving forward recovery for executing missing steps from a save-point (Garcia-Molina and Salem 1987). In the classical dependability taxonomy, however, compensation denotes masking an error through redundancy (Avizienis et al. 2004); this chapter follows the saga usage, so compensation counts as backward recovery even though each compensating step is itself a forward operation.

Garcia-Molina, Hector, and Kenneth Salem. 1987. “Sagas.” Proceedings of the 1987 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 249–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/38713.38742.
Avizienis, Algirdas, Jean-Claude Laprie, Brian Randell, and Carl Landwehr. 2004. “Basic Concepts and Taxonomy of Dependable and Secure Computing.” IEEE Transactions on Dependable and Secure Computing 1 (1): 11–33. https://doi.org/10.1109/TDSC.2004.2.

12.4.1 Recovery actions and evidence

Treating every failure as a blanket instruction to “try again” either re-sends a non-idempotent provisioning call after an ambiguous timeout or tears down a healthy test environment env_r18_01 when only a local configuration field had the wrong type. Separating “try again” by the evidence each failure leaves behind yields five distinct choices, because forward repair splits into two forms while retry, compensation, and escalation each remain one. A transport retry repeats the same logical operation under an established repeat contract. Forward repair takes two forms: resampling requests an entirely fresh candidate proposal from the model, while feedback-based repair (Madaan et al. 2023; Shinn et al. 2023) supplies targeted diagnostics to prompt a revised proposal. Compensation executes registered actions to establish fallback postconditions, and escalation transfers decision authority or unresolved obligations to an authorized supervisor. Table 2 separates these choices by the evidence they require and the obligations that remain on the runtime.

A network timeout raises an effect-status question before any model-repair question can be addressed, and the runtime relies on the settlement rule to resolve whether external state was altered. Similarly, an HTTP 429 rate-limit response proves only what the external service contract guarantees; it does not prove that an attempted state mutation was rejected before execution. Exponential backoff regulates the timing and throughput of a permitted retry, but no backoff schedule can transform an unsafe, non-idempotent repeat into a safe operation.

In contrast to an ambiguous timeout, a pre-dispatch configuration rejection provides a well-defined basis for repair. Because the schema validator rejected the payload locally, the runtime establishes that the proposed action never reached the executor or modified remote state. The runtime can therefore supply the original request, the target schema, the field-level diagnostic, and the task scope within a bounded repair prompt, validating the revised proposal before admitting any side effect. If the repair instead modifies source code rather than an ephemeral test configuration, the modified artifact constitutes a new candidate revision requiring fresh test evidence; previous test passes for ws_r18 cannot certify altered code.

↩︎ Structured, bounded rejection diagnostics are established in Rejection diagnostics.

4 Measured self-repair: When code-generation repair was evaluated against unconditioned resampling under matched sampling budgets, performance gains were often modest, varied substantially across problem subsets, and occasionally disappeared; high-quality feedback from a stronger verifier or a human supervisor yielded the only substantial improvements (Olausson et al. 2024). In the absence of external ground-truth feedback, unguided self-correction on complex reasoning tasks can actually degrade accuracy (Huang et al. 2024). Because the diagnostic signal rather than the retry attempt provides the corrective value, engineers must estimate \(\RepairSuccessProbability\) separately for each failure class and feedback mechanism.

Olausson, Theo X., Jeevana Priya Inala, Chenglong Wang, Jianfeng Gao, and Armando Solar-Lezama. 2024. “Is Self-Repair a Silver Bullet for Code Generation?” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Huang, Jie, Xinyun Chen, Swaroop Mishra, Huaixiu Steven Zheng, Adams Wei Yu, Xinying Song, and Denny Zhou. 2024. “Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Providing feedback modifies the model’s prompt conditioning without expanding its execution authority. While drawing independent resamples from a fixed prompt yields the same success probability on every attempt, repeated failures in operational agents are rarely independent. Instead, repeated failures typically expose an intrinsically difficult task, a persistent prompt misconception, or an unexpected environment state. The empirical repair success rate \(\RepairSuccessProbability\) is therefore a measured property of a specific failure class and feedback policy, which systems engineers must estimate from recorded operational episodes.4

↩︎ The feedback axis established in Revising from observations along the feedback axis separates feedback that adds an external observation from asking the model to re-examine its own output.

Accumulating failed responses and diagnostic traces across successive attempts also inflates the prompt for subsequent calls. Runtimes must manage two distinct resource boundaries: the per-invocation context window capacity and the cumulative task token meter. Context assembly must fit relevant diagnostic evidence within a single invocation’s context limit, whereas the billing meter charges for all input and output tokens consumed across the full sequence of repair calls. While context compaction can summarize historical attempts to fit within the context window, compaction consumes additional model calls, adds latency, and discards fine-grained diagnostic detail. Notebook 12.3 in section 12.6.2 prices what appending feedback turn after turn costs under a concrete workload.

12.4.2 Admissibility and expected cost

A repair call that is inexpensive on average can still overrun the task’s remaining dollar ceiling in the worst case or attempt to bypass an expired permission grant. Before evaluating the economic trade-offs of recovery, the runtime therefore eliminates any candidate action that fails admission control. For instance, an authentication failure never grants permission to synthesize an unauthorized bypass; instead, the runtime must either request an authorized credential refresh, locate a permitted fallback operation, or pause execution and escalate. Financial, latency, and context limits enforce hard ceilings: the runtime admits a candidate repair only if its maximum bounded liability fits within the remaining budget and deadline allowances and the assembled repair context fits within the target model’s context capacity.

↩︎ Bounded-liability admission, which admits an operation only when its maximum charge fits the remaining allowance, is established in Reservation and settlement.

Expected cost guides the recovery policy only among candidate actions that have cleared admission. Historical expenditures are sunk costs; recovery decisions must depend strictly on forward-looking costs and risks: the cost of retaining useful state, rebuilding that state from scratch, attempting repair, and managing secondary failure. While expensive earlier preparation often implies costly reconstruction, past spending alone never justifies continuing down a failing path. Ongoing resource holding fees, unsafe residual side effects, or task deadline expirations often dictate that discarding partially completed work is preferable to attempting a repair.

12.4.3 Repair vs. rebuild

When env_r18_01 is already running and only the test configuration failed local validation, tearing down and rebuilding the environment wastes every second spent provisioning it. Yet spending turns on a low-probability repair wastes tokens and still pays for the rebuild if the repair fails. To compare attempting a bounded repair against executing an immediate fallback that unwinds and rebuilds the required state, let \(\FallbackCost\) denote the future cost of compensating existing side effects and rebuilding to a declared readiness endpoint. Let one bounded repair attempt cost \(\RepairAttemptCost\), with probability \(\RepairSuccessProbability\) of reaching that same endpoint. Assuming that an unsuccessful repair leaves the original fallback pathway available at unchanged cost, the expected total cost of attempting repair before falling back is

\[\mathbb{E}[\RepairFirstCost] = \RepairAttemptCost + (1-\RepairSuccessProbability)\FallbackCost. \tag{3}\]

For any strictly positive fallback cost, attempting repair first yields a lower expected cost than an immediate fallback whenever

\[\RepairSuccessProbability > \RepairSuccessProbability^* = \frac{\RepairAttemptCost}{\FallbackCost}. \tag{4}\]

Engineers must evaluate this comparison independently for latency (in seconds) and monetary expense (in model dollars), as their respective break-even thresholds \(\RepairSuccessProbability^*\) often diverge. If \(\RepairAttemptCost \ge \FallbackCost\), no success probability below one can make repair cheaper on that resource axis. Furthermore, if a failed repair alters residual side effects, extends resource holding times, or increases fallback complexity, the term \((1-\RepairSuccessProbability)\FallbackCost\) must be replaced by the corresponding conditional fallback cost. Expected cost is the last of three gates, not the first. Evidence class decides which candidates exist at all, admissibility removes those that authority, allowance, or the deadline forbids, and only then does equation 4 rank the survivors; figure 2 lays out that order.

Napkin Math 12.1: Repair vs. unwind at matched readiness
Problem: After the locally rejected integration-test configuration, at what repair success probability does one attempt become cheaper than immediate compensation and rebuilding, measured separately in time and model dollars? Both paths end at the same endpoint: a usable isolated environment and a validated configuration ready to submit the integration test against ws_r18, possibly in a different environment after rebuilding.

Variables: All inputs are hypothetical, and the earlier operations are settled, so no unknown provisioning request is being repeated. Immediate compensation takes 40.6 s; rebuilding, including obtaining and validating a usable configuration, takes 46 s. Rebuilding uses 38,500 input and 3,100 output tokens at $3 and $15 per million, respectively. Compensators use no model tokens.

The isolated repair call receives 2,390 tokens in its entire request, including instructions, scope, failed configuration, and diagnostic; no earlier conversation is silently appended. It produces 180 output tokens. Assume 0.09 s of pre-output processing for this short request, which excludes queueing and so is not a measured time to first token, then 50 tokens/s, followed by 0.4 s of local validation on either outcome. The local repair has no external effects; on failure its private candidate is discarded and the original fallback remains available at unchanged cost. Success means the stated readiness checks passed. Dollars cover model tokens only, and time excludes queueing and new outages.

Math: The fallback takes \(\FallbackCost\) = 40.6 s + 46 s = 86.6 s and $0.162 in model charges. One repair attempt takes \(\RepairAttemptCost\) = 0.09 s + 180/50 s + 0.4 s = 4.09 s and costs $0.00987. The break-even \(\RepairSuccessProbability^* = \RepairAttemptCost/\FallbackCost\) is about 4.7 percent for elapsed time and 6.1 percent for model dollars. At an assumed 50 percent success probability, \(\RepairAttemptCost + (1-\RepairSuccessProbability)\FallbackCost\) gives an expected 47.39 s and $0.09087.

Result: Repair first pays on both axes whenever at least one attempt in 16 succeeds, and at the assumed rate it cuts expected time to readiness by 45.3 percent.

Systems insight: The fallback cost \(\FallbackCost\) sets the decision threshold. When compensation discards expensive, reusable preparation, the ratio \(\RepairAttemptCost/\FallbackCost\) is small, making repair the preferred first attempt for any failure class whose measured success rate clears it. As \(\FallbackCost\) decreases toward \(\RepairAttemptCost\), however, the threshold approaches certainty and immediate unwind becomes the optimal choice. Expected cost ranks admissible actions; the admission check still decides which actions are permitted to run.

12.4.4 The repair episode

Even when a single repair attempt is cheap enough to try first, allowing an agent to retry a broken configuration without a cap, or to reset its attempt counter by renaming the step, turns a bounded fix into an open-ended loop. An agent runtime cannot know in advance whether a given failure is repairable, so it groups corrective attempts into a bounded repair episode.5 Each episode maintains a unique identity, records the task and candidate versions it governs, and tracks an attempt counter against an explicit allowance, the repair cap \(\RepairCap\). Every corrective model invocation or tool dispatch must pass admission control and reserve its liability against that allowance. Because the counter increments upon admission rather than success, failed, timed-out, or aborted executions cannot evade the limit. Furthermore, transport retries and secondary proposals remain explicitly tied to their parent operations, ensuring that underlying retry mechanisms cannot silently consume a fresh allowance.

5 Recovery blocks: Randell’s classic recovery block architecture structured fault tolerance around a primary routine, an acceptance test, and a fixed sequence of alternates, each executed from a restored checkpoint until one passed or the sequence was exhausted (Randell 1975). An agentic repair episode preserves the acceptance test and finite iteration, but its alternates are dynamically sampled from a language model rather than written in advance. Consequently, exhausting an episode signals an exhausted recovery budget rather than proof that no feasible solution exists.

Randell, Brian. 1975. “System Structure for Software Fault Tolerance.” IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering SE-1 (2): 220–32. https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.1975.6312842.

Because the attempt limit is an operational policy parameter, exhausting it provides no proof that the underlying problem is unsolvable. A task’s cumulative financial budget and outstanding obligations carry across any revised plan or renamed operation. Only an authorized policy modification can initiate a new repair episode, and that new episode inherits all previous telemetry and resource consumption.

Observing repeated errors or identical candidate states justifies early runtime inspection, but repetition alone does not constitute definitive proof of non-progress. An unchanged error diagnostic can follow valuable internal context pruning, and revisiting an earlier state may represent a deliberate search backtrack. Section 12.6 formalizes dedicated watchdog mechanisms to detect these stalls. Once the episode allowance is exhausted or an admission check fails, the runtime denies further repair attempts and selects among still-permitted compensation, a paused continuation awaiting operator inspection, or an explicit escalation.

Checkpoint 12.2: Choose and bound a repair

A disciplined recovery strategy evaluates evidence class first, verifies admission constraints second, and optimizes expected cost last.

The break-even analysis in section 12.4.3 assumed that compensation remains an available fallback pathway throughout repair. Certain external commitments, however, irreversibly alter outside state, permanently foreclosing compensation and demanding different safety guarantees.

12.5 Irreversible Commitments

When an agent abandons an in-flight repair task, releasing the temporary integration-test environment env_r18_01 deallocates its virtual infrastructure while leaving behind only the accumulated compute charges. Publishing a software package to an external registry or rolling a configuration change across a live production fleet crosses a different boundary, because no recovery action within the agent’s authority can pull back downloaded artifacts or undo dropped customer requests. The property of reversibility is therefore always relative to a specified subsystem and an explicit boundary of control: a local filesystem snapshot restores the directories it tracks and nothing beyond them, and a cloud teardown stops future billing without erasing past logs.

12.5.1 Recovery contracts

A runtime cannot infer how to undo an action from an endpoint’s name or its coarse authority tier alone: a configuration change that can be reverted by restoring a snapshot in a local workspace requires an explicit compensating call in a staging environment and becomes unrecallable once pushed to live client traffic. Before initiating any state modification, the execution runtime must therefore determine three properties for each proposed effect: the precise target state, the recovery action available under the task’s granted authority, and the concrete evidence needed to verify that the recovery postcondition holds. Table 3 distinguishes three operational recovery contracts. These contracts represent intrinsic semantic properties of the underlying operation rather than superficial endpoint names or the coarse authority levels \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\) to \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\).

Tearing down a temporary test environment sits squarely in the second row. Releasing environment instance env_r18_01 deallocates its underlying compute resources, but earlier execution history and billing charges remain permanently on record. When a workflow instead includes an action in the third row, one that no operation within the agent’s authority can undo, the runtime can no longer rely on post-hoc compensation and must organize the plan around the point of no return.

12.5.2 The commitment region

Suppose an authorized publishing agent deploys an artifact to an external package registry first and checks its cryptographic signature and dependency versions second. When the signature check fails, the defective package is already publicly visible across external mirrors, and no action within the agent’s authority can withdraw copies that downstream users have already downloaded. An irreversible action in the third row of table 3 therefore requires explicit architectural structure, because the recovery planner must organize surrounding preparation and verification around it.6

6 Pivot subtransaction: The term comes from multidatabase transaction models, which classified each subtransaction as compensatable, retriable, or pivot (neither), allowed at most one pivot per global transaction, and required commits in the order compensatable, pivot, retriable (Mehrotra et al. 1992). The commitment region keeps that order: compensable preparation first, one pivot, then follow-up duties that can only be retried or handed off.

Mehrotra, Sharad, Rajeev Rastogi, Abraham Silberschatz, and Henry F. Korth. 1992. “A Transaction Model for Multidatabase Systems.” Proceedings of the 12th International Conference on Distributed Computing Systems (ICDCS), 56–63. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICDCS.1992.235055.
Definition 12.3: Pivot action

A pivot action is the designated irreversible commitment within a bounded region of a plan, after which restoring the region’s precommitment state is no longer an available recovery strategy.

  1. Significance: The pivot splits a plan region into two distinct phases: a compensable preparation phase before it and an irreversible phase after it. Hoisting validation checks ahead of the pivot is the primary systems mechanism for preventing defective actions from becoming permanent damage.
  2. Distinction: Being a pivot is an architectural property of an action within a plan region under a specific authority grant, rather than an intrinsic attribute of an API endpoint name. The same API call may be compensable under an administrative role with rollback privileges, yet act as a pivot under a restricted operational grant.
  3. Common pitfall: Delaying checks until after the pivot when they could have run before it. Executing validation late converts failures that compensation could have cleanly resolved into committed production outages.

A commitment region encompasses the span of an execution plan structured around a single pivot: preparatory work that compensation can still unwind, the pivot action itself, and the follow-up obligations that remain afterward. Commitment regions carry intent before effect (principle 11) across a step that compensation cannot reach. Everything the runtime must check and record is settled before the irreversible action.

Within each commitment region, the agent must perform every validation check that can legitimately precede commitment before releasing the irreversible action. The runtime must preserve all necessary artifacts, validate their versions and dependencies, and record the authorized intent, resource reservations, recovery metadata, and follow-up obligations to durable storage under the failure-domain contract of Acknowledgment and the failure domain. Only after crossing this durable boundary does the pivot action face its final admission check at dispatch time.

Limiting each region to a single pivot is an architectural discipline rather than a restriction that an entire task trajectory contain only one irreversible action. When a complex workload requires several independent state changes, the plan decomposes into a sequence of distinct commitment regions.

In that publishing workflow, suppose the agent must both release the approved artifact to the external registry and notify downstream recipients. A naive plan publishes the artifact first, validates signatures and dependency versions second, drafts release notes third, and sends notifications fourth, exposing the public registry to any defect that step 2 catches. Table 4 reorders the workflow so that every local check and compensable preparation step runs ahead of publication, isolating publication as the single commitment of its region and placing recipient notification in a second region that waits for publication evidence.

True data and control dependencies bound how aggressively the runtime can hoist operations. For example, a validation check that depends on the published artifact, such as confirming that a public package mirror resolves its new identifier, cannot run before release. That verification must remain after the commitment, tracked as an explicit operational risk assigned to a designated owner.

Executing a commitment does not terminate cleanup. When an agent allocates independent temporary resources that are no longer needed by subsequent duties, the runtime must still release them. The orchestrator keeps the list of pending compensations across the commitment boundary, withholding only those recovery actions whose preconditions no longer hold or whose execution would directly contradict the committed state.

The software repair agent encounters few pivots within its typical grant, because even submitting a pull request for review can be withdrawn or closed. In contrast, the on-call remediation agent encounters a pivot in nearly every incident it handles, because it operates at authority level \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\) against shared production infrastructure.

Lighthouse 12.1: Remediation agent (pivot and compensation)
Workload: The remediation agent responds to production alerts when a service’s error rate spikes following a configuration change. Its task contract permits querying telemetry systems, staging a candidate configuration on a canary slice (the small fraction of servers that receives traffic first), and rolling that configuration out fleet-wide after obtaining approval. Its operational horizon is tight, and any state modification directly affects live traffic across shared production infrastructure.

Recovery contracts: Querying telemetry metrics and reading logs produces no state changes to compensate. Staging a correction on the canary slice is fully compensable; reverting the canary restores the declared routing and configuration predicates, even though client requests already processed by the canary cannot be recalled. Scaling server replicas is compensable, leaving behind only the accumulated cloud charges. Rolling the configuration across the fleet alongside a rolling service restart constitutes the commitment region’s pivot action, because severed TCP connections, invalidated cache entries, and requests dropped during the restart lie beyond any recovery action permitted by the agent’s grant.

Binding constraint: While the software repair agent’s recovery budget is dominated by token re-reads during prompt reconstruction, the remediation agent’s recovery cost is dominated by expected irreversible loss. Every diagnostic check and synthetic test that can evaluate the canary belongs before the fleet rollout. Furthermore, the human-in-the-loop approval gate described in Approval Gates must sit directly at this pivot boundary, maximizing the operational loss prevented per minute of engineer review.

The lighthouse’s ordering rule can be priced. The expected irreversible loss of an execution plan, \(\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss\) (the subscript is part of the symbol’s name, not an authority level), sums a product over every proposed action \(\PlanAction\). The product combines the probability that the action is defective, its blast-radius consequence if committed, and the unrecoverable fraction \(1-\ActionRecoverableFraction\) that compensation cannot restore:

\[\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss = \sum_\PlanAction \Pr(\text{bad}\ \PlanAction)\cdot \text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\cdot(1-\ActionRecoverableFraction). \tag{5}\]

After reordering, changes the canary misses dominate the remaining loss.

Hoisting a diagnostic check ahead of the pivot transforms an unrecoverable failure mode (\(\ActionRecoverableFraction = 0\)) into a partially compensable canary rollback (\(\ActionRecoverableFraction > 0\)). Conversely, scheduling that same validation check after the pivot leaves \(\ActionRecoverableFraction = 0\), forfeiting any loss reduction. A real pre-pivot check recovers through two separate factors. It catches only a fraction \(\PrePivotCatchRate\) of defective actions, and reverting a caught action avoids only a fraction \(\RevertFidelity\) of its consequence, while a missed action recovers nothing. The action’s recoverable fraction in equation 5 is therefore the product \(\ActionRecoverableFraction = \PrePivotCatchRate\,\RevertFidelity\), and keeping the factors apart shows which one an engineering investment moves.

Napkin Math 12.2: Expected irreversible loss around a pivot
Problem: How much expected irreversible loss (\(\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss\)) per task does hoisting canary verification ahead of the fleet rollout eliminate for the remediation agent, and how does a specified increase in the pre-pivot catch rate change the remaining loss?

Variables: A proposed fleet change is wrong with probability \(\DefectiveActionProbability\) = 4 percent. A wrong change that commits at the pivot inflicts \(\text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\) = $12,000. Canary checks catch \(\PrePivotCatchRate\) = 80 percent of wrong changes before the pivot, and reverting the canary avoids \(\RevertFidelity\) = 90 percent of a caught change’s impact. The attempt itself costs $20 across model inference calls, telemetry queries, post-change health checks, and on-call engineer review, with reviewer time accounting for the dominant share. All values represent scenario assumptions.

Math: Executing the fleet rollout first provides no recovery mechanism; equation 5 yields \(\DefectiveActionProbability\,\text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\) = $480. Staging on the canary first alters the risk: caught changes cost \(\DefectiveActionProbability \PrePivotCatchRate\,\text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\,(1-\RevertFidelity)\) = $38.40, while missed changes cost \(\DefectiveActionProbability(1-\PrePivotCatchRate)\,\text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\) = $96, totaling $134.40. These two terms collapse algebraically to \(\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss = \DefectiveActionProbability\,\text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\,(1 - \PrePivotCatchRate\RevertFidelity)\), which is equation 5 with \(\ActionRecoverableFraction = \PrePivotCatchRate\RevertFidelity\).

Result: Reordering operations cuts expected irreversible loss by 3.6×. Of the residual loss that remains, 71.4 percent stems from wrong changes that crossed the pivot undetected.

Systems insight: Because \(\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss = \DefectiveActionProbability\,\text{consequence}(\PlanAction)\,(1-\PrePivotCatchRate\RevertFidelity)\), each 10 percentage points of pre-pivot catch rate eliminate $43.20 of expected loss per task, roughly 2× the attempt’s baseline execution cost. Even after reordering, the remaining loss is still 7× that attempt cost. For high-authority \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\) workloads, \(\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss\) belongs directly in the expected cost calculation of equation 1.2. Charged to every attempt, accepted or not, the loss joins the numerator, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask = (\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost] + \ExpectedIrreversibleLoss)/\AcceptanceProbability\), where it dominates model token expenses. The demonstrated catch-rate sensitivity makes better canary coverage a candidate engineering investment. Choosing it ahead of better revert fidelity, fewer defective proposals, or a smaller consequence requires comparing their feasible expected-loss reductions against implementation cost and added delay. Improving revert fidelity benefits only caught changes; cheaper model calls change attempt cost rather than this scenario’s irreversible-loss term.

12.5.3 Local staging and external settlement

When a commitment region finishes its pivot action and must trigger a follow-up obligation on a second external service, crashing between the two calls can leave the first commitment complete while its required follow-up vanishes from memory. The runtime bridges that gap, without pretending the two external services share a transaction, by staging the follow-up obligation in the same local commit that records the pivot’s outcome.

Example 12.1: What an outbox does not commit
Scenario: Consider again the reordered publication workflow. Publishing the package and notifying external recipients represent two distinct commitments, where notification strictly depends on the verified outcome of publication.

Diagnosis: Once evidence of publication arrives, the runtime can atomically record that outcome while enqueuing the downstream notification into an outbox, a durable local log of outbound requests awaiting delivery. Because both state updates participate in a single local database transaction and write-ahead log, a system crash cannot persist the publication outcome while losing the notification obligation. Conversely, a crash immediately prior to that local transaction loses no state, because the durable publication intent and follow-up requirements survive, allowing recovery reconciliation to replay the update. This local database transaction cannot, however, span physical network boundaries, so it cannot atomically publish to the external registry and deliver a message to an external notification service.

If package publication succeeds but notification delivery fails, the orchestrator reports the partial outcome and retains the notification obligation for subsequent delivery. If the network drops the acknowledgment for the publication request itself, the settlement protocol applies directly to the publication operation. Under these conditions, the workflow engine can claim neither confirmed publication nor safe abandonment.

Systems lesson: The transactional outbox pattern renders pending external actions recoverable by persisting them as durable local obligations, while preserving the independent failure domain of each remote service.

An outbox entry that is recorded but not yet confirmed sent establishes only that an intent was committed to local durable storage, not that it was never transmitted across the network. A system crash or network timeout can occur immediately after an outbound request is sent but before the local database records the dispatch update. Consequently, the external settlement rule applies to a dispatched commitment just as it does to any other network operation. At a pivot boundary, while an operation’s outcome remains indeterminate, the runtime must suspend any subsequent action that relies on either verified commitment or verified noncommitment. Independent observation queries and safe local cleanup may proceed under their own admission checks, but the state machine must treat an unknown outcome as a distinct third branch, neither safely before the pivot nor definitively after it.

12.5.4 The finalization contract

While idempotent interfaces ensure that retrying an operation is safe, extended downstream outages or network partitions can outlast an agent’s permitted execution window. To prevent open-ended execution loops, the runtime establishes a finalization contract before executing any irreversible commitment. The contract extends the harness’s finalization reserve, which funds finalization on every exit path. Written for one commitment region before its pivot, the contract names what that funding must buy there: the allowable remediation procedures, bound resource allocations, hard execution deadlines, and the designated escalation targets for any unresolved obligations.

When normal task execution ends, whether by completing its primary plan, hitting a deadline, or exhausting its operational authority, the harness’s guards move the trajectory into the FINALIZING state. The finalization phase operates under a dedicated, separately metered resource budget. While model-driven repair steps must still pass standard admission checks, pre-registered deterministic cleanup handlers execute under their own restricted, highly targeted cleanup grants.

↩︎ The FINALIZING state, through which every exit path passes under a dedicated reserve, is established in Finalization and remaining duties.

When automated finalization fails to establish the required postconditions, the runtime writes a complete handoff record to durable storage. This record captures all verified state changes, indeterminate external operations, active resource leases, unreleased reservations, and the next responsible owner. The runtime then initiates a formal, acknowledged handoff. When an on-call engineer acknowledges the escalation page, that response transfers operational custody of the incident, leaving the underlying external operations to be reconciled manually.

Commitment regions and finalization contracts bound what a trajectory can damage. They do not bound what it can waste. A repair loop whose every call is admissible can consume its budget without moving the task toward completion, and no error ever announces it.

12.6 Semantic Watchdogs

In the running connection-pool repair, after the targeted check on revision r18 fails, the agent can oscillate between two complementary edits that each undo the other and reproduce the exact same test failure. Every API invocation succeeds, every tool execution exits cleanly, and the execution trace lengthens, yet the underlying defect remains untouched.

Classical liveness monitoring7 cannot catch this failure because a traditional heartbeat detects only the absence of activity: daemons that stop emitting keepalives, threads deadlocked on mutexes, or network connections stalled on unacknowledged packets. In those traditional failure modes, an external supervisor intervenes by restarting the stalled process. A looping agent trajectory fails in the opposite way, staying hyperactive and reporting green health status on every probe while compute tokens and API budgets burn. Hard cumulative caps eventually halt the runaway process, but only after the trajectory has consumed nearly its entire budget. Catching unproductive cycles early requires monitoring task-level semantic evidence rather than low-level process liveness.

7 Liveness property: In Alpern and Schneider’s formalization, a liveness property says that something good eventually happens, and every finite execution can still be extended to satisfy it (Alpern and Schneider 1985). No finite window of observations can therefore prove that a trajectory will never make progress. The runtime converts the question into a safety property, such as “no more than \(\AdmittedAttemptCap\) admitted attempts,” which a finite history can violate and a gate can enforce.

Alpern, Bowen, and Fred B. Schneider. 1985. “Defining Liveness.” Information Processing Letters 21 (4): 181–85. https://doi.org/10.1016/0020-0190(85)90056-0.

A semantic watchdog is a runtime policy that monitors task-level observations, flags indicators of stagnation or oscillation, and executes a bounded intervention. Because semantic signals represent heuristic, fallible evidence about task health rather than formal proofs of futility, the runtime decouples detection from execution control. The detector provides the diagnostic signal, but the runtime enforcement mechanism imposes the admission hold, suspending subsequent tool execution even when the model proposes continuing. That hold is the harness’s pending_control hold, a flag on the trajectory record that withholds admission of new work. The watchdog, control, and incident holds of this chapter all use that one flag and differ only in whether the trajectory then steps from READY into PAUSED. The split applies trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) to progress. The runtime, not the trajectory, decides whether work continues, because the trajectory cannot be relied on to notice that it has stopped advancing.

Static limits, such as the repair cap on a single repair episode or cumulative token and financial budgets, bound worst-case resource exposure across the entire trajectory. The harness already ends a runaway retry loop once its next call no longer fits the budget, and it steers, restricts, and then finalizes as headroom shrinks. Those stages respond to consumption, and consumption says nothing about progress. A semantic watchdog supplies the missing evidence, providing earlier, dynamic termination based on empirical progress. Even if an agent still possesses 80 percent of its turn allowance, continuing a detected non-progress pattern wastes both time and money. Watchdog heuristics operate strictly within existing budget envelopes; a permissive or malfunctioning detector can never grant an agent more attempts than its hard budget ceilings permit.

↩︎ The runaway retry loop and the steer, restrict, and finalize stages that respond to consumed budget are established in Staged budget escalation and enforcement.

12.6.1 Progress evidence

A watchdog that watches only raw tool return codes or uncanonicalized file timestamps either misses an agent toggling between two broken patches in ws_r18 or raises a false alarm while the agent runs read-only log inspections. To evaluate trajectory health accurately, a semantic monitor requires three components: a concrete projection of task state, an authoritative observation stream, and a sliding comparison window. In our automated defect-repair scenario, meaningful evidence includes the source tree modifications, the specific regression suites executed against each candidate revision, and the resulting compiler or test diagnostic streams. Systems designers must maintain strict provenance for this evidence: a passing unit test validates only its scoped assertions, not complete task correctness. The runtime attaches the source revision, test scope, and verifier configuration to every evaluation record, ensuring that the watchdog compares identical verification criteria across successive turns.
