Three complementary signals capture the primary failure patterns observed in practice. Table 5 summarizes what each signal detects alongside its operational failure modes.

To detect state recurrence, the runtime extracts the candidate workspace state, canonicalizes its contents, and computes a cryptographic or stable non-cryptographic digest. Let \(\CurrentStateDigest\) denote the digest of the canonicalized projection captured at observation step \(\TurnIndex\), and let the runtime retain a bounded history window of the previous \(\RetainedDigestCount\) digests. The watchdog flags a recurrence of period \(\StateRecurrencePeriod\) whenever the current digest matches an earlier entry in the active window:

\[\exists\, \StateRecurrencePeriod \in \{1, \dots, \RetainedDigestCount\}:\ \CurrentStateDigest = \LaggedStateDigest. \tag{6}\]

A period of \(\StateRecurrencePeriod=1\) indicates an uninformative stutter turn, where an admitted action produced no net change in the tracked workspace files. A period of \(\StateRecurrencePeriod=2\) captures an oscillation where the agent toggles back and forth between two candidate patches. Listing 1 implements equation 6 by scanning backward from the most recent observation.

Digest equality confirms that the captured projection has matched an earlier state, but it does not tell the runtime why. In practice, false alarms stem from productive investigative actions that leave workspace files unaltered, such as diagnostic read-only commands or log inspection, far more often than from hash collisions. Conversely, an agent can defeat an uncalibrated detector by writing a transient timestamp or updating an ephemeral scratch file on every turn, producing novel hashes while trapped in a conceptual loop.

Effective canonicalization therefore requires a precise specification: the runtime must explicitly declare which file paths, metadata attributes, and environment variables belong to the task projection, and filter out non-deterministic noise. Because file serialization, path sorting, and hashing scale linearly with total workspace size, state capture often dominates the runtime overhead of the watchdog. Maintaining a bounded history window of size \(\RetainedDigestCount\) caps memory consumption and comparison latency to \(\mathcal{O}(\RetainedDigestCount)\), though it inevitably blinds the detector to long-period cycles exceeding \(\RetainedDigestCount\) turns.

Verification metrics demand similarly rigorous interpretation. System designers typically orient evaluation metrics so that higher values denote better performance, for example by tracking the negative count of compilation errors alongside the number of passing unit tests. A naive watchdog policy might treat any upward movement in a single metric as sufficient evidence of progress. A more robust criterion requires a Pareto improvement, where at least one metric advances and no tracked metric regresses. Both rules have blind spots under real development workloads. A productive refactoring might temporarily break compilation while an agent restructures an interface, whereas a superficial patch might fix a trivial test while masking a severe regression in unmeasured components. Runtime policies therefore combine metric thresholds with declared grace periods, permitting temporary regressions within a bounded number of turns.

Action comparison operates prior to tool dispatch, whereas outcome comparison evaluates the empirical results of admitted operations. Re-submitting an identical command immediately after it returns a deterministic execution error provides strong evidence of stagnation. However, identical tool invocations are entirely legitimate when external preconditions change, such as polling a long-running batch job or re-reading an updated file. Conversely, minor variations in prompt formatting or parameter nonces can easily evade exact-string matching. While semantic embedding models can identify paraphrased tool arguments, they introduce inference latency and classification errors of their own. Reliable watchdogs anchor their logic directly on observed tool signatures and structured execution returns, which the runtime controls authoritatively, rather than on the model’s internal reasoning tokens. In the running repair, for example, observing that workspace ws_r18 remains byte-identical while background task test_r18 executes does not indicate a repair loop. The runtime tracks the pending asynchronous job and respects its execution deadline rather than demanding source modifications on every poll.

12.6.2 The cost of an undetected loop

Re-sent input grows with the square of turns; late stops pay for it.

Every extra turn a detector waits before flagging an execution loop costs more than the turn before it, because stateless foundation model APIs re-submit the accumulated conversation history, tool inputs, and environment responses on each call. If an agent loops without context pruning or prefix cache reuse, input tokens accumulate quadratically with the number of turns, the append-only resend of equation 2.9 applied from the loop’s first turn. Notebook 12.3 prices this compounding overhead by contrasting a trajectory governed solely by a generous turn ceiling with one terminated promptly by a semantic watchdog.

↩︎ Prefix cache reuse across turns is established in Prefix Caching Across Turns.

Napkin Math 12.3: What an undetected loop costs
Problem: Assume a loop continues to a 50-turn ceiling unless the runtime intervenes after completed turn 4. How much model spending and elapsed loop time does the intervention avoid? The intervention turn is stipulated, not a guaranteed first-detection time.

Variables: The first input has 1,000 tokens. Each turn appends 300 output tokens and an 800-token error observation, increasing the next input by 1,100 tokens. There is no compaction or prefix caching. The largest input plus output is 55,200 tokens, within the assumed 64,000-token combined capacity. Each serial turn takes 10 s despite the growing input, and hypothetical prices are $3 and $15 per million input and output tokens.

Math: equation 2.9 gives the total prompt tokens sent, \(\PromptTokensSent = \TurnHorizon\,\BasePromptLength + \TurnAppendLength\,\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon-1)/2\), with base prompt length \(\BasePromptLength\) and per-turn append \(\TurnAppendLength\); here \(\TurnHorizon\) counts completed turns from the loop’s start rather than the whole trajectory’s horizon. The ceiling case processes 50 \(\times\) 1,000 + 1,100 \(\times\) 50 \(\times\) 49/2 = 1,397,500 input tokens, costs $4.42 in model charges, and lasts 8.33 min. The early stop processes 4 \(\times\) 1,000 + 1,100 \(\times\) 4 \(\times\) 3/2 = 10,600 input tokens, costs $0.050, and lasts 40 s.

Result: The ceiling case costs about 88.7× as much, so earlier stopping avoids $4.37 of gross model charges.

Systems insight: Append-only re-sending makes a loop’s input grow with the square of the turns it runs, so detection latency is the primary engineering control; a static budget ceiling serves merely as an ultimate backstop. Stopping this loop at turn 25 instead of 50 already cuts its input 3.9×. In an end-to-end deployment, every dollar spent on an undetected looping attempt is absorbed into \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\) in equation 1.2. Because that expected attempt cost is divided by task acceptance probability, uncontained loops aggressively inflate the effective cost per completed task across the entire fleet. The saving is gross; detector inference overhead, false alarms on useful work, and compensating cleanup actions must be debited against these savings, while prompt caching and context compaction mitigate the steepness of the quadratic curve.

12.6.3 Watchdog responses

If a watchdog responds to a detected loop only by appending a warning to the prompt, the model can ignore the message and submit the same failing tool call again; if the watchdog instead aborts the trajectory on the first repeated digest, a false alarm kills a repair that was legitimately backtracking. To enforce intervention without turning every heuristic trigger into a task failure, the runtime constructs a structured diagnostic finding (recording the triggering observations, candidate workspace revisions, plan version, active detection rule, and sliding window indices) and asserts the pending_control hold on the trajectory record, which immediately blocks the admission gate from dispatching any further model-proposed tool calls. Unlike an operator pause or a security quarantine, however, the watchdog leaves the trajectory in READY rather than transitioning it into PAUSED, because two of the three available responses resume autonomous execution immediately after adjusting the trajectory’s context or workspace. While the admission hold is active, the runtime policy selects an intervention from the three escalation options detailed in table 6. Two of these options parallel the budget-escalation stages in the agent harness, although they are triggered by lack of progress evidence rather than by consumed budget: bounded diagnostic continuation injects corrective feedback just as the harness’s steer stage does, and ending model-directed repair moves the trajectory into guarded finalization just as the harness’s finalize stage does. The remaining option, controlled backtrack, has no counterpart in budget escalation because exhausting a budget cannot be fixed by restoring an earlier workspace checkpoint. Figure 3 models this detection and containment sequence. The top panel identifies a period-2 oscillation between alternating revisions (\(\CurrentStateDigest = \OscillationPriorStateDigest\)) that produce identical test failures. The bottom panel records a structured finding that clamps the trajectory into an admission hold, routing execution to diagnostic continuation, controlled backtracking, or finalization.

↩︎ Control plane state machine rules and admission holds are established in Trajectory States.

In a bounded diagnostic continuation, the runtime injects an explicit, factual watchdog observation into the agent’s context rather than terminating the session. A representative observation states: “The proposed test invocation was withheld. The candidate patch and test failure match an earlier attempt in the active observation window, and no changed preconditions were recorded. Formulate an alternative hypothesis before re-submitting.” This message reports concrete runtime evidence without speculating on model capability, entering a freshly assembled prompt rather than interrupting an in-flight token stream. The diagnostic prompt is not a suggestion; the admission gate enforces the boundary, rejecting repeated dispatches if the model ignores the feedback.

If intervention arrives during active work, the runtime records the same hold and withdraws eligibility from in-flight output before the continuation reaches a paused boundary. In distributed environments, initiating cancellation is an asynchronous request rather than proof that remote execution has terminated. Outstanding side effects, background test runs, and cloud infrastructure charges remain tracked under the settlement rules until remote services confirm termination, and any further investigation must pass the admission check.

When policy dictates a controlled backtrack, the engine applies checkpointing mechanisms to restore the workspace to a known-clean state. The runtime preserves the diagnostic history of the abandoned branch so the agent does not repeat the exact sequence, revalidates evidence for the restored candidate, and enforces command restrictions at the admission gate rather than relying on prompt steering. If the trajectory exhausts its retry or diagnostic budget, the runtime halts model execution entirely and transfers control to the guarded finalization protocol.

Checkpoint 12.3: Progress evidence and intervention

A watchdog turns fallible progress evidence into an enforced hold. Test both halves.

A progress monitor operates strictly within the scope of an individual trajectory. In production clusters, however, repeated execution failures often reflect an impaired upstream dependency (an overloaded database, a rate-limited package repository, a failing authentication service) shared across thousands of concurrent agents. Because individual attempt budgets do not limit aggregate cluster load, independent agents retrying in parallel can trigger a retry storm. Mitigating shared degradation requires shifting operational control from individual agent watchdogs to system-level circuit breakers and shared dependency boundaries.

12.7 Tool Circuit Breakers

When an external dependency such as an automated test harness or a code-execution sandbox slows down under load, multiple concurrent agent trajectories queue up behind it. A watchdog timer can suspend an individual stalled trajectory, but other trajectories continue submitting tests to the same saturated service, while underlying client libraries inject retries beneath the agent’s high-level proposals. System recovery therefore requires bounding aggregate load across the entire dependency pool rather than policing the execution limits of individual tasks.

A service slowdown does not, by itself, imply an increasing request rate. For a closed population of sequential clients, longer round-trip latencies depress the arrival rate of new requests because each client waits longer between turns. Work amplification emerges instead through feedback loops. Automated retries multiply outbound attempts. An abandoned timeout leaves orphaned work executing on the remote server while a replacement job begins. Uncoordinated middleware layers duplicate retries in nested stacks, and widespread simultaneous errors synchronize the subsequent wave of arrivals.8 An agent runtime must identify which mechanism is driving downstream load, rather than treating every timeout as an invitation to fire off another attempt.

8 Metastable failure: Retry amplification can outlive its trigger. Bronson and colleagues describe systems in which a brief overload causes failures whose retries keep offered load above capacity after the original cause has gone, so the system stays degraded until load is shed or the retry policy changes (Bronson et al. 2021). A shared tool dependency serving many trajectories is exposed to the same feedback loop.

Bronson, Nathan, Abutalib Aghayev, Aleksey Charapko, and Timothy Zhu. 2021. “Metastable Failures in Distributed Systems.” Proceedings of the Workshop on Hot Topics in Operating Systems (HotOS ’21), 221–27. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458336.3465286.

Containing these feedback loops requires pairing three complementary controls across the dependency boundary. When a dependency is actively failing, a circuit breaker intercepts and withholds new outbound calls based on recent infrastructure health signals. For calls that remain eligible to retry, a single retry owner applying exponential backoff with jitter spaces out and desynchronizes the attempts. While calls are in flight or queued, bulkheads cap the concurrency and queue slots that any one dependency can occupy on the host. None of these controls establishes whether an earlier in-flight operation, such as the unconfirmed test_r18 execution, ever ran to completion on the remote host.

12.7.1 Breaker scope and failure classification

If a single circuit breaker covers an entire multi-tenant tool service and counts every client schema error as an outage, one malformed agent trajectory can trip the breaker and lock out every healthy tenant in the fleet. A circuit breaker avoids that collateral outage by enforcing a dynamic admission policy over a clearly delineated dependency boundary (Nygard 2007). This boundary, the breaker’s scope, may correspond to a specific remote API endpoint, an entire geographical region, an isolated compute cluster, or a single tenant’s quota partition. If a downstream service returns a quota exhaustion error for one tenant, a tenant-scoped breaker trips only for that tenant’s traffic rather than cutting off access for the entire fleet. The breaker’s configuration determines which failure modes count against its health budget, the sliding observation window and minimum sample size required before taking action, and the protocol for probing whether the dependency has recovered.

Separating infrastructure failures from client errors is what keeps that health budget accurate. An outcome should count against the dependency’s health only when it reflects an inability of the service to handle work, such as dropped TCP connections, transport timeouts, internal server errors, or explicit throttling directives targeted at that scope. Conversely, a client or domain rejection returned by the scoped endpoint can be neutral for this breaker’s infrastructure-failure metric even when the task fails. A returned schema error, permission denial, authentication failure, or ordinary “record not found” response establishes that the responding endpoint handled that request; it does not establish that every operation of the dependency is healthy. A rejection produced by local validation or authorization before dispatch supplies no observation of the dependency and must not enter its health window.

For the policy used here, the window includes dispatched calls with classified outcomes at this dependency scope, including endpoint-generated client/domain rejections as nonfailure observations. Locally withheld proposals are excluded. Other policies may exclude neutral endpoint responses as well, but must state that denominator explicitly. Formally, consider a rolling window \(\BreakerOutcomeWindow\) of recent classified outcomes. Let \(\CountedFailureIndicator_i = 1\) if call \(\BreakerOutcomeIndex\) was a counted infrastructure failure and \(\CountedFailureIndicator_i = 0\) otherwise. The breaker trips to the OPEN state when

\[\lvert \BreakerOutcomeWindow\rvert \ge \BreakerMinimumSampleCount \quad\text{and}\quad \BreakerFailureFraction = \frac{1}{\lvert \BreakerOutcomeWindow\rvert}\sum_{i \in \BreakerOutcomeWindow} \CountedFailureIndicator_i \ge \BreakerTripThreshold, \tag{7}\]

where \(\BreakerMinimumSampleCount\) represents the minimum evidence threshold required to prevent early spurious trips during periods of low traffic, and \(\BreakerTripThreshold\) is the policy’s failure-fraction threshold.

Table 7 adapts the classic three-state machine to tool-call admission. These gate-level states belong to one dependency scope and are distinct from the trajectory states that the control plane governs.

Tripping the breaker to OPEN governs subsequent call admissions only; it leaves requests that have already crossed the network boundary to resolve under their existing transport and settlement rules. The runtime records a withheld request as rejected at the gate, not as dispatched. The gateway returns a structured observation naming the blocked scope and the estimated cooldown, which lets the model revise its plan or choose another tool, but containment rests on enforcement at the gateway, not on the model heeding that message.

The HALF_OPEN state needs concurrency control. Under a single-probe policy, the gateway claims the probe permit atomically before dispatch; otherwise dozens of workers waking at the end of a cooldown would each act as the probe and re-saturate the recovering dependency. Each trial cycle also increments a monotonic breaker_generation counter, recorded with the probe’s identity at admission, and only a response carrying the active generation may change the breaker’s state. The counter is local fencing metadata, distinct from a trajectory’s control_epoch and from an authenticated storage owner_epoch.

↩︎ Fencing tokens and the owner_epoch that a replacement worker installs at enforcing boundaries are established in Availability and ownership.

If a dispatched probe times out or fails, the breaker immediately reopens to OPEN and increments breaker_generation. Robust policies also lengthen the subsequent cooldown interval via exponential backoff:

\[\BreakerCooldownInterval \leftarrow \min(2\,\BreakerCooldownInterval,\, \MaximumBreakerCooldownInterval),\]

ensuring that an uncooperative or persistently degraded dependency receives geometrically fewer probe attempts up to the ceiling \(\MaximumBreakerCooldownInterval\). If a delayed response from an earlier probe generation eventually succeeds, that late arrival can settle its own operation record and its charges, but the generation guard prevents it from closing the newly reopened breaker or usurping a newer trial’s slot.

A probe is a concrete network operation, not a cost-free diagnostic abstraction. It must pass normal admission checks and follow a safe operational contract. A supported read-only status query serves well as a probe; issuing a resource-creation request under the label of a health probe risks creating orphaned remote state. Moreover, because a successful probe proves only that the service handled a single isolated request, the gateway must restore regular traffic gradually under rate-limiting and concurrency constraints. Figure 4 illustrates transitions across the three breaker states (CLOSED, OPEN, HALF_OPEN). Failure rates trip the breaker, atomic probe slots govern trial execution, generation counters fence out late responses, and consecutive probe failures double the cooldown interval (\(\BreakerCooldownInterval\)) up to its maximum ceiling.

12.7.2 Retry coordination and jitter

Three independent retry layers turn one failing call into 27 attempts.

When a client library, an intermediate API gateway, and an agent planning loop each retry a failing call on independent allowances, their retries multiply downstream load just when the dependency is failing. If each of those 3 layers allows 3 attempts (one try plus two retries) on its own allowance, a single failing logical operation sends 27 requests downstream, one for every path through the 3 layers. A single owner holding the same allowance would send 3 (Brooker 2019). The multiplier grows exponentially with the number of layers; delegating retry authorization to a single designated component and accounting for attempts across all layers in one shared budget removes it.

Before scheduling any retry, the governing component must apply the settlement rules and idempotency contracts. The system preserves the original logical operation identifier while distinguishing individual network transport attempts. If the agent mutates the request payload, for example by adjusting a flag after an intermediate reflection step, the runtime treats the invocation as a brand-new logical operation rather than a retry attempt. Once scheduled, the retry must pass through all admission checks exactly like fresh traffic; waiting out a backoff window confers no privileged bypass of gateway admission policies.

When multiple requests fail simultaneously during a service disruption, retrying after identical, fixed delays preserves their synchronization, causing recurrent traffic spikes at each retry step. Capped exponential backoff with full jitter9 breaks this synchronization by doubling the backoff ceiling exponentially up to a maximum cap and drawing the actual delay uniformly at random:

9 Binary exponential backoff: The idea predates service retries. In the 1976 Ethernet design, a station whose transmission collided waited a random interval whose mean doubled after each successive collision (Metcalfe and Boggs 1976). Randomization desynchronized contending senders and doubling adapted to unknown load, the same two jobs jitter and the exponential cap do for retries against a shared tool.

Metcalfe, Robert M., and David R. Boggs. 1976. “Ethernet: Distributed Packet Switching for Local Computer Networks.” Communications of the ACM 19 (7): 395–404. https://doi.org/10.1145/360248.360253.

\[\RetryBackoffInterval_j = \min(\MaximumRetryBackoffInterval, \RetryBackoffInterval_0 2^\RetryAttemptIndex), \qquad \RetryJitterDelay_j \sim \operatorname{Uniform}(0,\RetryBackoffInterval_j). \tag{8}\]

Here \(\RetryAttemptIndex=0\) indexes the first retry, \(\RetryBackoffInterval_0\) represents the base backoff duration, and \(\MaximumRetryBackoffInterval\) enforces an upper bound on the backoff ceiling. Brooker’s analysis of retry strategies demonstrates that full jitter minimizes client contention and total completion times compared to uniform or decorrelated alternatives (Brooker 2015). The parameter \(\MaximumRetryBackoffInterval\) caps the span of individual delay intervals rather than the total number of attempts. Furthermore, if a downstream dependency returns explicit backoff metadata (such as an HTTP Retry-After header), that server-specified minimum overrides any shorter delay sampled locally.

While independent jitter alters the arrival distribution and suppresses average contention, it supplies no deterministic reduction in peak burst size because random draws can cluster and coincide with background traffic. Rate-limiting gates and concurrency bulkheads must therefore inspect every request following its delay, re-evaluating admission rules and breaker_generation before dispatch. When the breaker remains open, the gateway must reject the request cleanly, preventing the agent runtime from spinning in an unthrottled loop of failed retries and redundant model inferences.

12.7.3 Bulkhead isolation

Without per-dependency concurrency limits, one unresponsive tool, such as an overloaded code-formatting service, can tie up every worker slot in the host runtime until the agent can neither run local commands nor commit checkpoint state. Bulkhead isolation prevents a single stalled dependency from starving the rest of the runtime by partitioning system resources into segregated compartments with explicit concurrency and queue limits per external dependency (Nygard 2007).

Systems architects must distinguish between the specific physical resources being constrained. Blocking worker threads, asynchronous event handles, open TCP sockets, retained payload buffers, and remote job reservations represent different resource pools. Switching an agent engine from synchronous threads to asynchronous coroutines frees operating system threads, but an unresolved asynchronous call still consumes file descriptors, socket buffers, and heap memory for in-flight context. Effective bulkheads define explicit limits for each bottleneck resource.

Little’s law (theorem 5.1) relates mean occupancy to admitted throughput and mean slot residence in a stable system. If each admitted call holds one worker until the same external timeout and there is no other worker residence in this calculation, their product gives mean blocked workers. Applying the offered request rate instead estimates the worker demand the dependency would create if every request were admitted. For sustained smooth traffic, negligible scheduling gaps, and work-conserving service, clipping that demand at the pool size gives a fluid occupancy estimate. Here \(\ArrivalRate\) denotes the offered request rate, \(\BlockingCallTimeout\) the timeout, and \(\SharedPoolWorkerCount\) the pool size:

\[\BlockedWorkerCount = \min(\SharedPoolWorkerCount,\, \ArrivalRate \BlockingCallTimeout). \tag{9}\]

When offered demand reaches or exceeds the pool size, this approximation predicts saturation if enough dependency work remains queued to keep every worker busy. It does not determine exact occupancy or time to exhaustion under arbitrary arrivals, admission rejection, or competing local work. Without a dependency cap, those blocked calls can deny local work a worker even when the local operation itself is fast.

Bulkheads bound queue capacity as well as active concurrency. When both are full, the gateway fails fast, rejecting further requests at admission and reporting that disposition to the task’s recovery manager. A deeper queue absorbs transient bursts at the cost of memory and wait time, and no queued request may be dispatched after the task’s deadline has passed.

Local concurrency bounds do not constrain remote work that outlives a client-side timeout. Closing a local socket leaves the test or compilation running on the remote cluster, so bounding downstream blast radius means bounding outstanding logical operations and their liability, through cooperative cancellation or by withholding further submissions. Partitioned thread pools likewise do nothing about shared-disk or network saturation caused by concurrent jobs. A bulkhead protects only the quantities it explicitly limits.

Jitter lowers the expected retry rate, not the possible peak.

Napkin Math 12.4: What capacity and jitter actually bound
Problem: Which worker-occupancy limits can bounded admission guarantee in the stated burst, and which retry-arrival quantities does jitter determine only in expectation?

Occupancy: Suppose 256 trajectories each submit one blocking external call to an otherwise idle pool of 300 workers. No call finishes during the admission burst, and there are no nested calls. Without a dependency cap, only 44 workers remain free for everything else. This is the transient burst before any timeout fires; equation 9 gives the separately stated smooth-load estimate, while the explicit dependency cap bounds worker occupancy in either case.

Bounded admission: Now cap that dependency at 64 occupied workers and 32 waiting requests. Queued requests do not occupy workers. The burst fills both limits and rejects 160 requests before dispatch. It cannot occupy 236 workers in the shared pool. The cap protects worker slots, not CPU time or storage access.

Retry arrivals: Separately, consider 128 eligible retries released at one time, with no subsequent retries or unrelated traffic. If they all arrive in a stipulated 2 ms interval, the average over that interval is 64,000 requests/s. If each independently chooses an arrival uniformly across 2 s, the expected offered rate inside that interval is 64 requests/s. A fixed 2 ms subinterval has expected count 0.128; its realized count can be larger.

Systems insight: A cap provides a deterministic bound on concurrency, while jitter shapes only the expected arrival rate over time. With the cap in place, the stalled dependency can hold at most 64 workers regardless of how long the stall persists, making the cap size the maximum fraction of the pool that the dependency can ever monopolize. Jitter lowers the mean offered rate 1,000-fold, from 64,000 requests/s to 64 requests/s. That factor is the ratio of the jitter span to the stipulated burst window. The lesson lies in the gap between the expected count and the realized one, which can include every retry in the cohort, so peak rate containment still requires an admission rate limiter. Both mechanisms bound local host resources rather than remote liability.

Checkpoint 12.4: Contain load without erasing uncertainty

Breakers, caps, and jitter each bound a different quantity. Name the quantity before trusting the bound.

Breakers, retry ownership, and bulkheads protect a shared dependency from the agents that use it. When the suspect component is a trajectory itself, the question changes from how much load to admit to what may proceed at all.

12.8 Incident Containment

Suppose the automated repair agent attempts to send private test fixture data through an external package-search tool. The disclosure gate rejects the invocation before dispatch and places the trajectory on hold, shifting the runtime’s problem from protecting a failing dependency to protecting every downstream system, shared memory store, and artifact pipeline that the suspect trajectory has touched or might touch next. The immediate systems challenge is determining what may safely proceed while the control plane assesses the affected state.

↩︎ Disclosure through a permitted network tool, which leaks on the outbound query, is established in Disclosure Through Permitted Tools.

An isolated timeout, a rejected tool proposal, or the ingestion of an untrusted document does not by itself prove system compromise. The incident record must separate the observed violation from its suspected root cause and document why policy mandates a hold. Incident containment restricts subsequent activity and prevents the propagation of potentially corrupted artifacts, all while preserving forensic evidence and maintaining explicit responsibility for actions already committed. Containment operates as an operational disposition layered directly onto existing trajectory states rather than introducing an ad hoc state machine.

12.8.1 Holds on model-directed work

If an incident policy asks the suspected trajectory’s own model to clean up env_r18_01, it hands a potentially compromised context another chance to issue tool calls. Yet if it kills the worker without running cleanup, env_r18_01 stays live and unowned. Because sagas and compensating actions rely on deterministically registered runtime handlers and pre-authorized admission checks rather than the ongoing cooperation of the model, containment can safely halt all model-directed work while keeping registered compensation routines running. Placing an agent on hold upon suspicion applies the principle of fail-safe defaults (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975).10 When the evidence justifying further model-directed execution is in doubt, the default decision denies admission rather than granting it.

10 Fail-safe defaults: Saltzer and Schroeder argued for basing access decisions on permission rather than exclusion because the two designs fail differently: a mistake in a permission-based mechanism refuses a legitimate request, which someone notices and reports, while a mistake in an exclusion-based one grants access silently (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975). An incident hold inherits that asymmetry; a wrongly held trajectory costs time and surfaces in review, whereas a wrongly admitted action may be irreversible.

Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.

The supervisor registers the same pending_control hold a watchdog uses, immediately revoking eligibility from active outputs and held proposals. Unlike a watchdog hold, an incident hold does not wait for a policy choice. It carries the trajectory through the guarded pause entry established in Pause entry and outstanding work, from READY to PAUSED without leaving an unmonitored window of execution, while an unrecoverable task transitions directly through FINALIZING.

Containment decisions must be pushed all the way to the dispatch boundaries that enforce them. Revoking a capability grant inside the control record accomplishes nothing if a downstream dispatch adapter continues to honor an unexpired token. While suspending a local thread halts process execution on that node, it cannot claw back network packets already in flight, guarantee that a remote worker has ceased computation, or invalidate credentials already distributed to third-party endpoints. Actual cancellation and revocation depend on the operational semantics and APIs of the affected services. The supervisor must therefore record three distinct states: which restrictions were requested, which have been confirmed by downstream services, and which remain uncertain. A 2012 trading failure shows what happens when no such external hold exists and operators improvise recovery against a live production system.

War Story 12.1: Knight Capital's stale order path (2012)
Context: On 1 August 2012, Knight Capital’s automated equity order router, SMARS, handled retail orders for a newly launched exchange program. The deployment of the new code missed one of the router’s 8 servers, which still held an obsolete trading function, Power Peg, retired in 2003. The new software also reused the internal flag that had originally activated Power Peg (U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission 2013).

Mechanism: Orders carrying the reused flag reached the eighth server and triggered Power Peg, which lacked logic to track cumulative order fills. As a result, it continuously routed child orders for 212 parent orders that were already complete. Knight lacked controls to compare order volume leaving SMARS against orders entering it, possessed no mechanism to halt SMARS upon abnormal activity, and had no automated alerts hooked to its position monitor. Before the market opened, 97 automated emails reading “Power Peg disabled” arrived in employee inboxes, but staff disregarded them because the system did not categorize them as critical alerts. When operators intervened, they uninstalled the new code from the 7 servers where it had deployed successfully. This live rollback directed all subsequent incoming traffic into Power Peg across the entire cluster.

Impact: Over roughly 45 minutes, the router executed more than 4 million trades across 154 stocks, totaling more than 397 million shares. Knight accumulated a net long position of approximately $3.5 billion and a net short position of $3.15 billion, suffering a realized loss of $460 million.

Fix: Under an SEC settlement, Knight hired an independent consultant to review its software deployment pipelines, order router architecture, pre-trade risk controls linking capital thresholds to order entry, and incident response procedures.

Systems lesson: Two recovery failures compounded an initial deployment defect. Because the operators lacked an external halt mechanism decoupled from the routing engine, they attempted to debug and reconfigure SMARS while it was processing live trades. Furthermore, their improvised recovery action bypassed pre-flight admission checks, deploying the defective legacy path across all servers.

U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. 2013. “Securities Exchange Act of 1934, Release No. 70694: Knight Capital Americas LLC.”

The two failures map onto obligations of an agent runtime. First, containment controls reside in the external supervisor and are enforced at every tool-dispatch boundary; halting an agent trajectory must never depend on the cooperative shutdown of the agent itself. Second, recovery actions must be registered in advance and pass the same admission checks as forward progress, so an unvetted rollback cannot execute an untested code path. The control missing behind both, a comparison of orders leaving the router with orders entering it, is a conservation check that must live outside the component it checks. It is the same separation that keeps a semantic watchdog’s detector outside the model whose progress it judges. Table 8 organizes these containment obligations, which overlap in practice rather than executing in a strict sequence.

The obligations overlap, but the individual restrictions inside them still need an order whenever the incident owner can issue and confirm only one at a time. Until a restriction is confirmed, its source keeps exposing the system at some rate, as when a rollout keeps changing hosts, a dispatch adapter keeps forwarding queued requests, or other trajectories keep retrieving a tainted artifact. Because all unconfirmed sources continue leaking in parallel, each restriction accumulates exposure at its source’s rate across both its own confirmation time and the confirmation times of every restriction placed ahead of it. Spending a minute confirming one restriction delays every remaining restriction by that same minute, so issuing first the restrictions that remove the most exposure per minute of confirmation time minimizes total accumulated exposure. Under that ratio rule, a fast restriction on a moderate leak can rightly precede a slow restriction on a severe one. Two safety constraints override that ranking. No restriction may destroy the incident record, and none may strand a registered cleanup by revoking the grant that cleanup needs.

To support post-incident analysis and remediation, the incident record links the triggering symptom to complete trajectory metadata: source code versions, proposed candidate patches, operation and retry attempt identifiers, capability grants, and resource consumption records. It explicitly identifies both the primary incident owner and the owners responsible for unresolved side effects. The runtime preserves this audit trail under its declared durability contracts. To prevent secondary information leaks, sensitive payload data must be routed to an access-controlled forensic evidence store rather than surfaced in general operational alert channels. If telemetry logging failed or dropped events during the execution window, the system must explicitly flag those capture omissions in the incident log rather than assuming clean silence.

12.8.2 Affected artifacts

Stopping a compromised trajectory’s next tool call does not stop another agent or a deployment pipeline from reading a summary, memory entry, or candidate patch that the compromised run already wrote. Incident quarantine therefore operates under different constraints than data provenance and confidentiality tracking. Provenance traces the origin of an object, and confidentiality restricts who may read it. An incident disposition governs whether a specific version of an artifact is trustworthy enough for continued operational use. For example, a trusted internal test fixture can remain strictly confidential without suffering data corruption, whereas a publicly generated task summary may require immediate quarantine if prompt injection embedded an adversarial instruction into its text.

⇄ Contrast: Integrity provenance and confidentiality, established in Integrity and confidentiality, govern where data came from and who may receive it; an incident disposition governs whether an artifact version may still be used.

When containment trips, the runtime computes a conservative dependency closure containing all potentially tainted artifacts, drawing on prompt context histories, workspace modifications, derived objects, and recorded tool calls. The moment a violation is detected rarely marks the initial point of contamination. When the audit log cannot establish a precise, minimal interval of compromise, the runtime expands the blast radius to include all potentially affected artifacts and explicitly logs that epistemic uncertainty. Every artifact within this conservative boundary remains frozen until verified by trusted evidence.

Quarantine policies must intervene at every interface where held artifacts could influence subsequent decisions: dynamic context assembly, vector memory retrieval, external artifact export, release gating, and subsequent repair attempts. Purging a compromised source document, such as a malicious README, is ineffective if a derived intermediate summary or an updated long-term memory embedding still reflects the injected instruction. The closure is the same provenance walk that cascading revocation performs on memory, extended here to workspace files, staged proposals, and pending approvals. Staged proposals and pending human approvals generated from tainted context must therefore be held as well. If engineers require access to suspect artifacts for diagnosis, the runtime can grant bounded inspection privileges without clearing the artifact for general execution.

↩︎ Cascading revocation of summaries, memories, and pending proposals derived from a compromised source is established in Persistence through derived records.

Publishing an incident hold does not recall artifacts already transmitted to external services. The incident owner must identify all recorded downstream recipients, dispatch secondary hold requests, and track explicit acknowledgments alongside any unconfirmed deliveries. While cryptographic signatures on a revocation notice verify the sender’s identity, they offer no guarantee that a remote downstream consumer has actually applied the restriction.

↪ Lineage checks that enforce revocation without relying on push delivery to every worker are developed in Revocation and lineage checks.

Releasing an artifact from quarantine requires auditable evidence tailored to both the artifact’s exact cryptographic digest and its intended operational purpose. An authorized human reviewer or a deterministic verification suite must document the validation criteria and findings; any release grant applies strictly to that specific revision and target scope. A generative model’s self-reported claim that an environment is clean carries no evidentiary weight. Furthermore, a candidate patch produced by a repair agent constitutes a brand-new artifact revision that must independently pass admission before deployment.

12.8.3 Residual settlement

Freezing model-directed work and quarantining suspect files still leaves any external cloud environment or in-flight test that the trajectory launched before the hold running and accruing charges. When audit logs explicitly bind the operation provision_test_env_r18 to resource identifier env_r18_01, a trusted recovery service can safely invoke release_test_env_r18 under an administrative cleanup grant once model-directed grants have been rescinded. This teardown succeeds only if the compensation call passes admission checks verifying the resource’s current identity, ownership, and dependent links. Conversely, if provisioning status remains ambiguous, containment offers no license to guess resource identifiers and issue speculative deletions. Any residual cleanup that the trajectory cannot complete within its allotted finalization deadline must be escalated, along with all accumulated audit telemetry, to an authorized recovery service or human operator.

Remediation strategies vary (restoring an isolated workspace snapshot, swapping out a corrupted artifact, executing compensating transactions), but every intervention must account for concurrent operations, concurrent users, and forensic data retention. Indiscriminately rolling back every touched file risks obliterating unrelated concurrent updates or erasing critical crash logs needed to diagnose the failure. System operators must define recovery actions by their desired postconditions and safety invariants rather than applying blanket rollbacks keyed merely to an incident tag.

Example 12.2: A blocked disclosure and an outstanding job
Scenario: The blocked package_search call that opened this section arrives while the earlier test_r18 invocation and the provisioning request provision_test_env_r18 are both still unsettled. The trajectory is now under an incident hold.

Diagnosis: Because the gate rejects the call before dispatch, no fixture bytes leave the host. That guarantee is structural, not the outcome of a race between process suspension and token streaming. The hold, however, changes nothing about the work already in flight. The operation table keeps the test_r18 invocation and the provisioning obligation open with their owners. When an authenticated completion packet for test_r18 arrives later, it settles that operation and its charges in the operation table, yet the trajectory stays held, because settling an earlier operation is evidence about that operation and not a release decision for the trajectory.

Systems lesson: Successfully blocking an outbound request proves only that the intercepted call caused no external damage; it provides no evidence regarding actions executed earlier in the run. Any prior network requests or external side effects remain unverified incident residuals requiring explicit ownership and investigation.

Checkpoint 12.5: Containment is not disappearance

Containment restricts future actions while preserving the historical record of prior execution. For each scenario, distinguish forward restrictions from forensic and residual obligations:

Each mechanism so far has answered one kind of failure in isolation. Real incidents arrive together, and the test of the design is whether state reconstruction, saga compensation, progress watchdogs, dependency controls, and containment compose into one protocol that can be audited and validated against injected faults.

12.9 Fault-Tolerant System Synthesis

A single turbulent hour during an automated connection-pool repair can exercise every recovery mechanism introduced across this chapter. In rapid succession, a cloud environment provisioning reply drops over the network, the executing worker node crashes along with its ephemeral local storage, the integration testing service throttles under load, and a malformed or adversarial repository document steers the agent’s next patch proposal. At each boundary the runtime establishes three facts: what durable evidence it holds, which state transition that evidence permits, and which unfulfilled obligations remain with an accountable owner.

12.9.1 Mechanism composition

If crash recovery, saga compensation, progress watchdogs, and circuit breakers each maintained separate state machines, a repair policy could bypass an open breaker during rollback or invent a missing test outcome after a failover. An append-only control record and authoritative event history tie these recovery subsystems together into one shared protocol. Ownership leases and worker lifecycle guards determine which process has the authority to act at any given instant, while the operation table holds every side-effecting action open until correlated evidence settles it. When an action must be rolled back or aborted, registered compensation metadata specifies the permissible corrective workflows. Meanwhile, dynamic execution budgets supply the exact runtime, token, and financial quotas required by upstream admission checks. Under this architecture, the repair policy cannot bypass admission rules or invent missing execution evidence by steering down an optimistic control-flow branch.

This composition assumes that durably acknowledged history and authoritative storage artifacts survive worker crashes and the loss of ephemeral local volumes. Return to the replacement worker that opened the chapter and the crash worksheet it inherits. The surviving runtime reads the published checkpoint at \(\CheckpointLogPosition\), which binds the record snapshot record_R_ckpt to the immutable workspace snapshot ws_r18. Folding the durable event suffix, it finds the admitted execution of test suite test_r18 under attempt attempt1 but no settlement or completion record, and the executor’s job handle job_tests_18 survives only if its acknowledgment was flushed before the crash. Rather than guessing the test outcome or dropping the unfinished task, reconstruction preserves test_r18 as an unresolved operation that still requires verification or compensation.

↩︎ The crash worksheet, with checkpoint record_R_ckpt, operation test_r18, and job handle job_tests_18, is established in Checkpoints.

12.9.2 Outstanding effects worksheet

A recovery runbook that assumes every interrupted check or teardown call eventually succeeds will silently promote a dropped test reply to a passing grade or mark an unconfirmed release as clean. Table 9 instead functions as a conditional design worksheet, where every proposed side effect must pass an explicit admission check and every reported outcome must satisfy formal settlement rules before the runtime advances.

A recovery sequence structured around this worksheet can terminate in several legitimate operational states depending on which evidence arrives. When an active worker recovers the missing result for test_r18, the repair workflow proceeds along its normal path. If configuration validation rejects a candidate patch and bounded repair fails, the runtime abandons that candidate and runs registered compensation to tear down temporary environments. A third outcome is neither completion nor clean teardown. If an external provisioning or release endpoint cannot confirm whether its operation took effect, the runtime halts forward progress and keeps the pending obligation open under an accountable owner. Leaving an unverified side effect explicitly unresolved keeps potential resource leaks visible instead of fabricating a clean slate.

Progress watchdogs, dependency breakers, and incident holds govern that same workflow whenever a failure raises no explicit error. If the agent repeatedly modifies connection-pool parameters without advancing its verification checks, the watchdog places the trajectory in an admission hold before it burns through its token ceiling. If the test service itself degrades under cluster load, a scoped circuit breaker blocks new outbound dispatches while letting previously forwarded provisioning requests settle. If the disclosure gate catches an injected instruction or a forbidden query, an incident hold freezes model-directed tool admission while still permitting preregistered cleanup compensations to finish under their narrow grants. Because all three controls act at the admission boundary rather than inside the prompt, none of them invents missing outcome data or widens the agent’s authority during a fault.

12.9.3 Fault-test plan

A recovery worksheet on paper cannot prove that the runtime preserves obligations when a reply drops or a scratch disk disappears, especially if tests only read the runtime’s own logs rather than inspecting external endpoints directly. A fault-testing harness turns the worksheet into observable system behavior by pairing isolated test fixtures with instrumented mock endpoints that record actual external state independently of the runtime’s self-reported logs. Before executing each test case, the test harness must explicitly define the target fault boundary, the relevant interface contracts, and the required terminal disposition. Failure domains must be distinguished with care: terminating a worker process while preserving its local disk tests crash recovery in an entirely different failure domain than severing the node and destroying its scratch volume.

Table 10 outlines the fault-injection scenarios needed to stress-test recovery contracts across these distinct domains. Test suites should isolate individual boundaries first to pinpoint recovery defects before combining multiple failures, such as dropping a provisioning response immediately before terminating the worker node, to verify that tracking obligations and compensation hooks survive complex failure cascades.

Test outcomes must be reported along separate dimensions rather than compressed into a single aggregate pass rate. These dimensions include task completion, established compensation postconditions, duplicate external effects, orphaned resources lacking an active owner, and unresolved outcomes. A single trajectory can contribute to several dimensions, so each reported rate must name its unit and denominator. Task completion rates include all attempted tasks, while compensation completion rates include all compensation obligations in the declared evaluation cohort, including those still unresolved; omitting unresolved cases produces artificially optimistic availability metrics. An execution trajectory can satisfy containment constraints while failing its assigned task, or it can produce a passing task check while leaving external infrastructure unreleased.

↪ A report template that retains failures and unavailable verdicts and records outstanding effects with their owners is developed in Failure Report and Regression Record.

Latency measurements during recovery require precisely named boundary timestamps: the interval from fault injection to detection, from detection to a guarded continuation or operator handoff, and from fault injection to verified resource settlement. When an operation remains unresolved at the test observation window’s cutoff, it has no meaningful settlement time. Engineers should report the raw count of unresolved cases rather than computing an average over resolved runs alone, which conceals tail latencies and hanging processes. Similarly, cost accounting must track recovery tool invocations, leaked or retained cloud resources, actual monetary charges, and outstanding reservations separately. In an agentic architecture, returning an idle worker to READY, receiving the first token from a language model, and establishing a verified, settled task result represent distinct operational milestones that must never be conflated.

Evaluating competing recovery policies requires strict experimental parity across benchmark tasks, injected fault distributions, endpoint semantics, execution authority, and resource allowances. Test harnesses must preserve complete execution traces alongside no-fault baseline runs. Without baseline controls, an evaluation cannot distinguish between a robust recovery mechanism and an over-defensive guardrail that repeatedly halts productive work.

↪ Paired comparison of two systems on the same sampled tasks under matched criteria and allowances is developed in Paired outcome comparison.

12.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Recovery errors in agentic architectures arise when engineers treat evidence gathered at one system boundary as an absolute guarantee about the state across another. Each fallacy or pitfall below mistakes a local record, a timeout, or an intermediate guard for settled external reality.

Fallacy: A local transaction or durable log makes a whole tool workflow atomic.

A local database transaction or write-ahead log protects only the state that directly participates in it, such as an internal outcome record and its corresponding outbox entry. Side effects produced by external tools remain outside that boundary, no matter how meticulously the runtime logs each invocation (section 12.2). Reliable recovery architectures must clearly delineate the true atomic boundary, treat downstream outcomes as unknown whenever execution halts ambiguously, and preregister compensation actions for every side effect that can escape into external systems.

Pitfall: Treating a timeout as permission to try a new operation.

When an external tool call times out, the earlier request often continues executing to completion on the remote service. For example, issuing a fresh provisioning request immediately after an earlier call times out can leave two live cloud environments running concurrently, one of them unowned, unmonitored, and accumulating billing charges. Systems must settle the definitive state of the original operation before proceeding, repeating requests only within the strict idempotency guarantees of that endpoint’s contract (section 12.4). Retrying an existing request, proposing a mutated alternative, and issuing a compensating transaction represent three distinct operations, each requiring its own explicit admission check.

Fallacy: Crossing a pivot guarantees forward completion and retires cleanup.

Crossing a pivot point commits the system to forward recovery and narrows remaining fallback options, but it provides no guarantee that subsequent operations will succeed. In the remediation scenario, even after canary checks approve a rollout to proceed, 71.4 percent of the remaining expected irreversible loss stems from erroneous modifications that slipped past the pivot undetected (notebook 12.2). These failures demand bounded follow-up procedures and designated human ownership, while outstanding cleanup tasks must remain durably recorded until explicitly settled.

Pitfall: Equating a repeated state or an open breaker with a resolved failure.

Observing an identical state digest does not necessarily indicate an unproductive loop; intentional search and backtracking algorithms generate the exact same fingerprint. Similarly, an open circuit breaker merely blocks new admissions within its protection scope while allowing in-flight external calls to continue executing. Runtimes must record precisely why an intervention took place and track which side effects remain unresolved. Enforcing execution boundaries at the infrastructural admission gate ensures that system constraints hold even when a planning model disregards an advisory prompt.

Fallacy: A cumulative budget ceiling is enough to catch a looping trajectory.

While a cumulative financial ceiling caps catastrophic expenditure, an agent trapped in an append-only context loop typically exhausts nearly the entire allocation before the threshold trips. In notebook 12.3, running an errant trajectory until it hits the 50-turn ceiling costs $4.42, roughly 88.7× the $0.050 incurred when halting at turn 4, because quadratic context accumulation inflates token volume on every turn. A cumulative ceiling functions merely as an emergency backstop, whereas active progress detectors with low detection latency provide the operational control.

Pitfall: Letting every layer retry on its own allowance.

When a client library, an API gateway, and a trajectory-level recovery orchestrator each independently permit 3 attempts, retries multiply across the system stack. A single failing logical operation can barrage downstream services with 27 requests instead of the intended 3, amplifying load precisely when an already degraded dependency is least able to absorb it. System designers must assign the retry allowance for each logical operation to a single authoritative owner that coordinates attempts across all 3 layers (section 12.7).

12.11 Summary

Recovering from failure in an agentic system requires making decisions under incomplete knowledge. An execution runtime cannot roll back the external world, nor can it always confirm whether an in-flight tool call actually took effect. Consequently, every recovery decision converges on two concrete questions evaluated at the admission boundary before the next operation runs: What has the observed evidence conclusively established, and what do current authority, dependencies, and resource allowances permit? Retries, forward repairs, backward compensations, pivot commitments, and external interventions are not disconnected subsystems. They are different answers to these two questions, which is why one operation table and one admission gate can govern them all.

Every quantitative comparison in the chapter set action on imperfect evidence against the cost of waiting for certainty. The pairs are an in-place repair attempt against a full rebuild and an early validation check before a pivot against irreversible loss committed after it. They also include a watchdog’s false interventions against runaway token burn and a rapid retry against the load it imposes on a struggling dependency. No analytical comparison relieves the runtime of maintaining explicit ownership over whatever state remains unsettled. Table 11 summarizes these quantitative relationships, which either establish the execution order for recovery actions or evaluate the trade-offs between competing recovery strategies.

Key Takeaways: Recover from evidence and preserve residual duties
  • A timeout settles nothing: An execution outcome is verified complete, confirmed absent, or explicitly classified as unknown under the endpoint’s contract. Neither an expired timeout, a logged intent, nor a restored local workspace can determine whether an external side effect actually took place. Consequently, an unknown outcome must keep its resource reservations locked and retain a designated owner until external reality is verified.
  • Cleanup is owed before the effect exists: The runtime must persist the compensation template (forward operation identity, blast-radius scope, dependencies, and identifier-binding rules) to durable storage before dispatching the forward action. Compensations are full operations with their own failure modes and outcomes, governed by an explicit cleanup dependency graph rather than an oversimplified reverse stack.
  • Repair pays only above its threshold: At any given endpoint, attempting a bounded in-place repair outperforms unwinding and rebuilding from scratch only when the measured success probability exceeds \(\RepairAttemptCost/\FallbackCost\), evaluated independently across latency and financial cost budgets. Expected statistical benefit must never bypass the admission gate, and every proposed repair action requires its own fresh evidence.
  • Irreversible loss is set before the pivot: Because compensation cannot undo the commitment in this scenario, moving the same validation check before the pivot allows it to avert loss that detecting the defect afterward cannot undo. In the remediation scenario, deploying a canary check prior to the pivot cut expected loss 3.6×. Both forward cleanup and residual obligations survive past the pivot.
  • Detection latency sets a loop’s cost: In an append-only agent conversation, prompt tokens grow quadratically with the turn count; allowing a cycling agent to reach turn 50 cost 88.7× as much as halting it at turn 4. Because intermediate progress signals are fallible evidence, the admission gate, not an advisory message to the model, must enforce the hold.
  • Retries need exactly one owner: Uncoordinated retry loops across layered subsystems multiply traffic exponentially just when a downstream dependency is failing. Containing a fault requires a single designated retry owner, generation-scoped circuit breakers, bounded probe requests, and per-dependency concurrency bulkheads. Randomized jitter lowers the expected arrival rate and concurrency caps bound worker occupancy, but only an admission rate limiter bounds the peak request rate.
  • Stopping a trajectory is not recovering it: Halting an execution trajectory does not eliminate active side effects, dangling external leases, or unsettled transactions. True containment maintains isolated, narrowly authorized cleanup routines, while robust fault injection tests evaluate residual duties and unverified outcomes rather than mistaking an idle process for a recovered system.

Intent before effect (principle 11) reaches past the intent record in this chapter to the cleanup that record implies. The runtime registers corrective obligations before dispatch, verifies its commitments before the pivot, and tracks every unsettled duty until evidence resolves it. Complementing this, trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) empowers the runtime to intervene and halt execution when progress metrics degrade. Systems engineers must design for the reality that tool actions cannot always be undone and tasks cannot always be saved. When failures strike, a well-architected agent runtime preserves safety, limits collateral damage, and maintains an auditable chain of custody over the state it leaves behind.

12.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The settlement rules used to reconcile agent actions descend directly from transaction processing. Gray’s foundational notes on database operating systems outlined how commit decisions span distributed nodes and explained why lost network packets inevitably leave one participant uncertain of the system’s global state (Gray 1978). In his classic 1981 essay on the transaction concept, Gray identified the practical limits that modern agent architectures still confront: workflows such as travel reservations touch external organizations, span days rather than seconds, and cannot hold locks until completion (Gray 1981). Instead, they rely on compensating transactions recorded by the coordinating system to undo committed side effects. Garcia-Molina and Salem structured this approach into the saga pattern, decomposing a long-lived transaction into a sequence of independently committed steps where each forward action is paired with a compensator (Garcia-Molina and Salem 1987). Multidatabase transaction models subsequently classified these steps by their recovery behavior, distinguishing between actions that can be compensated, actions that can be safely retried, and non-reversible pivot steps that determine whether the workflow commits or aborts (Mehrotra et al. 1992). Drawing on large-scale service designs that avoid a common distributed transaction across independently administered entities, Helland described coordination through tentative operations, explicit cancellation, and idempotent execution (Helland 2007).

A second lineage provided the conceptual vocabulary of software fault tolerance and distributed recovery. Randell introduced recovery blocks to give programs structured error handling, evaluating runtime results against an acceptance test and falling back to alternative algorithms from a restored checkpoint when an attempt failed (Randell 1975). Avizienis and colleagues later unified this discipline into a standard dependability taxonomy, formalizing the distinctions among faults, errors, and failures while defining rollback, rollforward, and compensation as primary recovery strategies (Avizienis et al. 2004). Foundations from distributed systems theory define the physical limits of these mechanisms. Alpern and Schneider established the formal boundary between safety and liveness properties (Alpern and Schneider 1985). Fischer, Lynch, and Paterson proved that no deterministic consensus protocol can guarantee termination while preserving agreement and validity in a fully asynchronous system where even one process may crash (Fischer et al. 1985). For agent systems, this impossibility result explains why an execution monitor cannot distinguish an abnormally slow tool from a crashed one. When an external service exceeds its latency budget, the coordinator cannot infer the outcome of the operation from network silence alone; it must treat the timeout as an operational deadline that triggers active status probing and state reconciliation.

Techniques for overload control migrated into agent execution from networking and cloud infrastructure. Metcalfe and Boggs introduced randomized exponential backoff in Ethernet to desynchronize colliding packet transmissions (Metcalfe and Boggs 1976). In distributed application architectures, Nygard popularized circuit breakers and bulkheads to isolate failing dependencies before resource exhaustion cascades through an entire fleet (Nygard 2007). Modern cloud engineering formalized the mathematical dynamics of these retry loops: Brooker analyzed how randomized jitter prevents synchronized retry waves and demonstrated how layered services multiply retry volume exponentially unless coordination is centralized (Brooker 2015, 2019). Bronson and colleagues demonstrated that uncontrolled retries can induce metastable failures, where an overloaded service remains trapped in degraded performance long after the initial transient fault has subsided (Bronson et al. 2021). Agent runtimes inherit these operational hazards and compound them with model-driven repair loops, where language models iteratively revise faulty tool calls or code based on compiler and runtime feedback (Madaan et al. 2023; Shinn et al. 2023). However, empirical studies by Olausson and colleagues (Olausson et al. 2024) and Huang and colleagues (Huang et al. 2024) show that the value of iterative repair depends on the failure class and diagnostic feedback. Repeated calls without informative feedback can consume compute budgets while reproducing correlated failures.

Nygard, Michael T. 2007. Release It!: Design and Deploy Production-Ready Software. Pragmatic Bookshelf.
Brooker, Marc. 2015. Exponential Backoff and Jitter. AWS Architecture Blog.
Brooker, Marc. 2019. Timeouts, Retries, and Backoff with Jitter. The Amazon Builders’ Library.
Madaan, Aman, Niket Tandon, Prakhar Gupta, Skyler Hallinan, Luyu Gao, Sarah Wiegreffe, Uri Alon, et al. 2023. “Self-Refine: Iterative Refinement with Self-Feedback.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 46534–94.
Shinn, Noah, Federico Cassano, Ashwin Gopinath, Karthik Narasimhan, and Shunyu Yao. 2023. “Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 8634–52.
  • Gray (1981) offers a concise and definitive explanation of why the traditional transaction concept breaks down for long-lived, cross-organizational workflows, establishing compensation as the essential recovery mechanism.
  • Garcia-Molina and Salem (1987) defines the saga and its forward and backward recovery strategies; reading it alongside this chapter highlights how uncertain tool execution complicates classical compensation models.
  • Helland (2007) explains from industrial practice how large-scale services reach agreement without distributed transactions, relying on tentative operations, explicit cancellation, and idempotent execution.
  • Avizienis et al. (2004) provides the foundational taxonomy of faults, errors, failures, and recovery techniques that modern dependability and resilience engineering build upon.
  • Bronson et al. (2021) frames retry storms and self-sustaining overloads, analyzing the failure regimes that circuit breakers and coordinated retry policies are designed to prevent.
  • Olausson et al. (2024) measures when model self-repair outperforms independent resampling under equivalent compute budgets, providing the empirical foundation for setting adaptive repair thresholds.
Helland, Pat. 2007. “Life Beyond Distributed Transactions: An Apostate’s Opinion.” Third Biennial Conference on Innovative Data Systems Research (CIDR).

12.13 Exercises

Each problem states its constraints and ends in a decision the runtime or its designer must make. Givens are hypothetical unless a lighthouse profile supplies them, and dollar figures cover only the charges each problem names.

1. Cleanup graph versus reverse stack (Basic). A branching repair workflow has five registered cleanup operations. Once admitted, ending the integration test takes 30 s, and preserving result artifacts outside the environment takes 20 s. Releasing the temporary environment takes 40 s and requires both of the first two to be settled. Revoking the environment’s temporary credential takes 5 s and requires the release. Deleting an unrelated private scratch directory takes 10 s and has no dependencies. Compute the elapsed cleanup time when a coordinator runs the five operations one at a time as a reverse stack, and when it runs the cleanup graph with unlimited parallelism. Then suppose the cancellation reply for the test is lost, so ending the test stays unresolved. Determine which operations can still settle, which are blocked, and what must be recorded for each blocked node.

2. Repair first on two axes (Basic). After a locally rejected test configuration, compensating and rebuilding to the same readiness endpoint takes 120 s and $0.40 in model charges. One bounded repair attempt takes 6 s and $0.03, and a failed attempt leaves the original fallback unchanged. Recorded episodes for this failure class succeed with probability 0.06. Compute \(\RepairSuccessProbability^*\) and the expected cost of repair first on each axis, and state which axis favors repair. Decide what the runtime should do when the task’s binding limit is its deadline and when it is its dollar allowance, and find the smallest measured success rate at which repair first pays on both axes.

3. Where a loop spends its budget (Basic). An append-only repair loop starts with a 2,000-token input. Each turn appends 400 output tokens and a 1,100-token error observation to the next input, with no compaction or prefix caching. The assumed combined input and output capacity is 64,000 tokens, and a cumulative ceiling stops the loop after 40 turns. A watchdog could instead intervene after completed turn 6. Compute the total re-sent input at the ceiling and at the intervention, and their ratio. Find the first turn by which the loop has sent half of its ceiling input, and check whether the context capacity would stop the loop before the ceiling does. Use these results to decide where engineering effort on loop cost belongs: the ceiling, the context limit, or detection latency.

4. Classify before tripping (Intermediate). A test-service breaker applies equation 7 with \(\BreakerMinimumSampleCount = 20\) and \(\BreakerTripThreshold = 0.5\) over the last 50 classified outcomes. The current window holds 14 transport timeouts, 6 server errors, 12 validation rejections of one trajectory’s malformed requests, 3 authorization denials, and 15 successful runs. The validation rejections and authorization denials were returned by the test-service endpoint, rather than by local pre-dispatch gates; this policy includes them as nonfailure observations in the window. (a) Compute \(\BreakerFailureFraction\) under the chapter’s classification rule and decide whether the breaker opens. (b) Recompute \(\BreakerFailureFraction\) for a breaker that counts every outcome other than a successful run as a failure, and identify who pays for that design. (c) The breaker has opened during a sustained outage. Each half-open probe resolves by a 2 s timeout, the next cooldown starts when the probe resolves, the first cooldown is 5 s, and each failed probe doubles the cooldown up to an 80 s cap. Count the probes sent in the first 600 s after the trip, compare the count with a fixed 5 s cooldown, and explain what the cap trades against.

5. Size the bulkhead (Intermediate). Logical test operations are offered at a steady 5 per second to a shared pool of 400 workers. During a sustained stall every admitted attempt holds a worker for its full 20 s timeout. All three retry layers exhaust their allowances, one try plus one retry per layer, wherever admission permits. Ignore backoff gaps for this worker-demand estimate. Use a smooth-load, work-conserving approximation, and distinguish the unconstrained attempt demand from the rate the finite pool can actually admit; waiting or rejected attempts do not occupy workers. A client library, a gateway, and trajectory-level recovery supply the three retry layers. (a) Compute the unconstrained attempt rate and worker demand. Estimate saturated occupancy when a persistent dependency backlog keeps the pool busy, and determine the maximum admitted attempt rate at that timeout. Explain why the entire offered retry stream cannot be admitted in steady state. (b) Repeat the demand and occupancy comparison with a single retry owner that allows one try plus one retry in total, under the same smooth-load assumptions. (c) Local tool work needs at least 150 workers at all times. Choose a dependency cap, and determine which of three levers (the cap, coordinated retries, or a shorter 10 s timeout) guarantees that reservation whatever the stall’s timeout turns out to be. (d) Under the coordinated policy, suppose all its offered attempts are admitted and each produces a distinct remote test, with no remote deduplication or cancellation and enough remote capacity for every test to run for 120 s. Estimate the mean outstanding remote jobs, and explain why the local cap does not bound them.

6. Price an approval gate at the pivot (Intermediate). Take the remediation agent’s profile from notebook 12.2. A proposed fleet change is wrong with probability 4 percent, and a wrong change that commits costs $12,000. Canary checks catch 80 percent of wrong changes before the pivot, and reverting a caught change avoids 90 percent of its impact. An additional human approval placed before the fleet rollout costs $20 of reviewer time per attempt and catches a fraction \(\ApprovalCatchFraction\) of the wrong changes the canary misses; a change caught at approval is reverted on the canary like any other caught change. (a) Write \(\ExpectedIrreversibleLoss\) with the gate as a function of \(\ApprovalCatchFraction\), and derive the break-even \(\ApprovalCatchFraction^*\) at which the gate pays for its reviewer cost. (b) Evaluate the net saving per task at \(\ApprovalCatchFraction = 0.5\). (c) The same reviewer placed after the rollout, auditing committed changes, catches the same fraction.
